Cartesian Linearly Distributive Categories: Revisited
Rose Kudzman-Blais, Jean-Simon Pacaud Lemay

Abstract
Linearly distributive categories (LDC) were introduced by Cockett and Seely to provide an alternative categorical semantics for multiplicative linear logic. In contrast to Barr’s ∗ \ast -autonomous categories, LDCs take multiplicative conjunction and disjunction as primitive notions. Thus, a LDC is a category with two monoidal products that interact via linear distributors. A cartesian linearly distributive category (CLDC) is a LDC whose two monoidal products coincide with categorical products and coproducts. Initially, it was believed that CLDCs and distributive categories would coincide, but this was later found not to be the case. Consequently, the study on CLDCs was not pursued further at the time. With recent developments for and applications of LDCs, there has been renewed interest in CLDCs. This paper revisits CLDCs, demonstrating strong structural properties they all satisfy and investigating two key classes of examples: posetal distributive categories and semi-additive categories. Additionally, we re-examine a previously assumed class of CLDCs, the Kleisli categories of exception monads of distributive categories, and show that they are not, in fact, CLDCs.



Acknowledgments.
The authors would first like to thank Richard Garner (who humbly declined to be a coauthor) for the many helpful, insightful, and productive discussions. The authors gratefully acknowledge the Department of Mathematics at Macquarie University for hosting the first author during a research visit to collaborate with the second author on this topic.

1
Introduction

Most categories of interest, whether from logic, theoretical computer science, algebra, or category theory more broadly, exhibit multiple monoidal products worth investigating. Within monoidal category theory, a significant body of research explores the distributivity of monoidal structures, that is, how two monoidal products interact. In general, this interaction is mediated by a natural transformation, known as a distributivity map, which may or may not be an isomorphism. Frequently, one or both of the monoidal structures is cartesian or cocartesian, meaning that monoidal structure is induced from finite products or coproducts respectively.

Perhaps the most well-known definition in this area is that of a distributive category
[
5
]
, a category with finite products and coproducts, whose products
\times
distribute over its coproducts
+
by an isomorphism:

\displaystyle(A\times C)+(B\times C)\xrightarrow{\cong}(A+B)\times C

(1)

This concept was generalized to distributive monoidal categories and extended further with the introduction of rig categories
[
19
]
, also known as bimonoidal categories
[
14
]
, which are categories equipped with two arbitrary monoidal structures whose distributivity is mediated by monomorphisms. They are named as such because they categorify rigs, modeling the distributivity of multiplication over sums in a ri(n)g.

Another important notion of a category with two monoidal structures is that of a duoidal category
[
1
]
, which arises canonically when a category has either finite products or coproducts. For a duoidal category, distributivity is a natural transformation given by an interchange:

\displaystyle(A\star B)\diamond(C\star D)\rightarrow(A\diamond C)\star(B\diamond D)

(2)

The theory of duoidal categories emerged in the study of braided monoidal categories, where the interchange law generalizes the canonical flip induced by braidings
[
16
]
.

The development of linearly distributive categories (LDC) fits naturally within this research program. LDCs were first introduced by Cockett and Seely in
[
6
]
as categorical semantics for multiplicative linear logic (MLL), as developed by Girard
[
12
]
. In this framework, the multiplicative conjunction
\otimes
and disjunction
\invamp
are taken as primitive notions, along with their interactions via linear distributors:

\displaystyle A\otimes(B\invamp C)\rightarrow(A\otimes B)\invamp C

(3)

This contrasts with
\ast
-autonomous categories, introduced by Barr
[
2
]
, which provide the categorical semantics for MLL with negation
[
20
]
, where multiplicative conjunction
\otimes
and negation
\ast
are taken as primitive, with the multiplicative disjunction
\invamp
defined via de Morgan duality. Consequently, linear distributivity is implicit, following from the duality between
\otimes
and
\invamp
. However, linear distributivity is a fundamental concept, as it precisely captures the interaction between two binary operations necessary to model the cut rule. The significance of explicitly considering linear distributivity is evident in the extensive and continuously growing literature on LDCs.

Initially, LDCs were referred to as weakly distributive categories, as linear distributivity (
3
) was thought to be a weakening of the classical distributivity of products over coproducts found in distributive categories (
1
). It was believed that specializing to cartesian linearly distributive categories (CLDC), which are LDCs where
\otimes=\times
is a product and
\invamp=+
is a coproduct, would recover the notion of a distributive category. However, this turned out to be false: linear distributivity and classical distributivity are, in some sense, orthogonal notions
[
8
]
. Following this realization, while research on LDCs has remained central to categorical linear logic, the study of CLDCs was not pursued further at that time.

With recent developments for and applications of LDCs, there has also been renewed interest in CLDCs. Thus, CLDCs remain an interesting structure worth exploring and deserving to be properly study. As such, the main objective of this paper is to revisit the theory of CLDCs and investigate various classes of examples. This work follows the first named author’s efforts to better understand the structure of CLDCs by developing a linearly distributive version of the Fox theorem
[
17
]
.

By investigating CLDCs more closely, we uncovered structural properties that had not been previously explored, revealing distinctive and nuanced behavior within CLDCs. One such key observation was that in a CLDC, its terminal object is always preinitial and, dually, its initial object is always subterminal. This implies that every CLDC is, in fact, a
mix
LDC. Furthermore, these mix maps can be used to show that, remarkably, in a CLDC, an object is preinitial if and only if it is subterminal.

In our investigation of examples of CLDCs, two key classes emerged: bounded distributive lattices and semi-additive categories. Indeed, it appears to often be the case that a CLDC must fall into one of these two categories via collapse theorems. There are already two well-known collapses of LDCs to posets: the first being Joyal’s paradox, and the second stemming from the orthogonality between linear and classical distributivity. In addition to these, we discovered two new collapse results: a CLDC must be semi-additive if it either has
invertible
linear distributivities or if it is
isomix
. At first glance, these collapses seem to constrain the landscape of possible CLDCs. In an effort of developing new examples, we introduce a Grothendieck construction that generate new examples of CLDCs, some of which are neither bounded distributive lattices nor semi-additive categories.

On the other hand, we also reconsider the Kleisli category of the exception monad of a distributive category, which were thought to be CLDCs according to
[
8
]
. However, thanks to our better understanding of CLDCs, it soon became apparent that something was amiss and that Kleisli categories of exception monads for distributive categories are not, in fact, examples of CLDCs. Nevertheless, they are still LDCs, where
\invamp
is still the coproduct, but where
\otimes
is the product on objects but defined differently on maps. As such, it follows that classical distributive restriction categories
[
11
]
are also LDCs (but not CLDCs).

The outline of this paper is as follows. In Section
2
, we carefully review the necessary background theory on LDCs and the morphisms between them. Section
3
revisits CLDCs, where we explore fundamental properties shared by all CLDCs and also develop the appropriate notions of morphisms between CLDCs. In Section
4
, the first significant class of CLDC examples is investigated: bounded distributive lattices. Section
5
examines the second key class of CLDCs: semi-additive categories. Two new collapse theorems are presented, along with a construction that maps a CLDC to a semi-additive category. Section
6
revisits the Kleisli category of the exception monad of a distributive category and explains why it is not a CLDC, despite still being a well-defined LDC. Finally, Section
7
discusses two simple constructions that provide further examples of CLDCs: products and fibrations.

Conventions:
Arbitrary categories will be denoted as
{\mathbb{X}}
or
{\mathbb{Y}}
. In an arbitrary category
{\mathbb{X}}
, objects are denoted by capital letters (
A
,
B
,
C
, etc.), while the maps are denoted by lowercase letters (
f
,
g
,
h
, etc.). Identity maps are denoted by
1_{A}
, while composition is denoted by
;
and written in diagrammatic order, that is, the composition of
f:A\to B
and
g:B\to C
is
f;g:A\to C
which first does
f
then
g
. For an arbitrary (symmetric) monoidal category, we denote it simply as a triple
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oslash,I)
where
{\mathbb{X}}
is the underlying category,
\oslash
is the monoidal product, and monoidal unit
I
, and with
\oslash
-associator
{\alpha_{\oslash}}_{A,B,C}\xrightarrow{\cong}(A\oslash B)\oslash C\rightarrow A\oslash(B\oslash C)
, right
\oslash
-unitor
{u_{\oslash}^{R}}_{A}\,\colon A\xrightarrow{\cong}A\oslash I
, and left
\oslash
-unitor
{u_{\oslash}^{L}}_{A}\,\colon A\xrightarrow{\cong}I\oslash A
(and
\oslash
-symmetry
{{\sigma_{\oslash}}_{A,B}\,\colon A\oslash B\xrightarrow{\cong}B\oslash A}
).

2
Background: Linearly Distributive Categories

Before introducing CLDCs, we first need to provide some important background on LDCs. Beyond their introduction in
[
8
]
, the theory of LDCs was further developed in a subsequent series articles by Cockett and Seely, sometimes alongside co-authors Blute and Trimble
[
3
,
7
,
9
]
. We refer the reader to those papers for an in-depth introduction to LDCs.

2.1
Linearly Distributive Categories

Our notation for LDCs follows more closely the notation introduced by Girard in
[
12
]
, in contrast to the notation used by Cockett and Seely in
[
6
]
. We have chosen to do so as to not conflict with other standard categorical notation. As such, multiplicative conjunction is represented by
\otimes
, known as tensor, with unit
{\bf 1}
, and multiplicative disjunction is
\invamp
, known as par, with unit
\Bot
.

Definition 2.1

[
8
, Sec 2.1]

A
linearly distributive category
(LDC) is a category
{\mathbb{X}}
equipped with two monoidal structures:

(i)

A
tensor
monoidal structure
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})
;

{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A,B,C}\,\colon(A\otimes B)\otimes C\rightarrow A\otimes(B\otimes C)\hskip 9.24994pt{u_{\otimes}^{R}}_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow A\otimes\top\hskip 9.24994pt{u_{\otimes}^{L}}_{A}\,\colon A\rightarrow\top\otimes A

(ii)

A
par
monoidal structure
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\invamp,\Bot)
;

{\alpha_{\oplus}}_{A,B,C}\,\colon A\oplus(B\oplus C)\rightarrow(A\oplus B)\oplus C\hskip 9.24994pt{u_{\oplus}^{R}}_{A}\,\colon A\oplus\bot\rightarrow A\hskip 9.24994pt{u_{\oplus}^{L}}_{A}\,\colon\bot\oplus A\rightarrow A

which also comes equipped with natural transformation:

\displaystyle\delta^{R}_{A,B,C}\,\colon(A\invamp B)\otimes C\rightarrow A\invamp(B\otimes C)

\displaystyle\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}\,\colon A\otimes(B\invamp C)\rightarrow(A\otimes B)\invamp C,

where
\delta^{R}
and
\delta^{L}
are called the left and right
linear distributors
respectively, such that:

(i)

The linear distributors are compatible with the unitors in the sense that the following equalities hold:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{u_{\otimes}^{L}}_{A\invamp B};\delta^{L}_{{\bf 1},A,B}={u_{\otimes}^{L}}_{A}\invamp 1_{B}\\
&{u_{\otimes}^{R}}_{A\invamp B};\delta^{R}_{A,B,{\bf 1}}=1_{A}\invamp{u_{\otimes}^{R}}_{B}\\
&\delta^{L}_{A,B,\Bot};{u_{\invamp}^{R}}_{A\otimes B}=1_{A}\otimes{u_{\invamp}^{R}}_{B}\\
&\delta^{R}_{\Bot,A,B};{u_{\invamp}^{L}}_{A\otimes B}={u_{\invamp}^{L}}_{A}\otimes 1_{B}\end{split}

(4)

(ii)

The linear distributors are compatible with the associators in the sense that the following equalities hold:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\delta^{L}_{A\otimes B,C,D};({\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A,B,C}\invamp 1_{D})={\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A,B,C\invamp D};(1_{A}\otimes\delta^{L}_{B,C,D});\delta^{L}_{A,B\otimes C,D}\\
&({\alpha_{\invamp}}_{A,B,C}\otimes 1_{D});\delta^{R}_{A\invamp B,C,D}=\delta^{R}_{A,B\invamp C,D};(1_{A}\invamp\delta^{R}_{B,C,D});{\alpha_{\invamp}}_{A,B,C\otimes D}\\
&{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A\invamp B,C,D};\delta^{R}_{A,B,C\otimes D}=(\delta^{R}_{A,B,C}\otimes 1_{D});\delta^{R}_{A,B\otimes C,D};(1_{A}\invamp{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{B,C,D})\\
&\delta^{L}_{A,B,C\invamp D};{\alpha_{\invamp}}_{A\otimes B,C,D}=(1_{A}\otimes{\alpha_{\invamp}}_{B,C,D});\delta^{L}_{A,B\invamp C,D};(\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}\invamp 1_{D})\end{split}

(5)

(iii)

The left and right linear distributors are compatible with each other in the sense the following equalities hold:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\delta^{L}_{A\invamp B,C,D};(\delta^{R}_{A,B,C}\invamp 1_{D})=\delta^{R}_{A,B,C\invamp D};(1_{A}\invamp\delta^{L}_{B,C,D});{\alpha_{\invamp}}_{A,B\otimes C,D}\\
&(\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}\otimes 1_{D});\delta^{R}_{A\otimes B,C,D}={\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A,B\invamp C,D};(1_{A}\otimes\delta^{R}_{B,C,D});\delta^{L}_{A,B,C\otimes D}\end{split}

(6)

It may be useful to draw out the commutative diagrams for some of the axioms of the linear distributors. For example, here are the first equality of (
4
), (
5
), and (
6
) respectively.

213.85474pt\raise-47.21873pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.89684pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta^{R}_{A,B,C}\invamp 1_{D}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 213.85474pt\raise-55.43748pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-41.46368pt\raise-62.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 89.11539pt\raise-68.20624pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.38124pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\alpha_{\invamp}}_{A,B\otimes C,D}}$}}}\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(A\invamp(B\otimes C))\invamp D}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

We leave drawing out the other axioms as commutative diagrams as an exercise.

In practice, one is often more interested in modeling commutative MLL. This is accomplished by considering
symmetric
LDCs.

Definition 2.2

[
8
, Sec 3]

A
symmetric linearly distributive category
(SLDC) is a LDC
{\mathbb{X}}
such that
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})
and
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\invamp,\Bot)
are symmetric, and such that the linear distributors are compatible with the symmetries in the sense that the following diagram commutes:

(7)

It is worth mentioning that for SLDCs, we can in fact suppress one of the linear distributors and simply define one from the other. For example, we can define a SLDC simply in terms of the left distributor
\delta^{L}
and define the right distributor
\delta^{R}
via (
7
).

2.2
Shifted Tensors

The simplest example of LDCs are simply monoidal categories themselves, where the linear distributors are the associativity isomorphisms of the monoidal product.

Example 2.3

Every (symmetric) monoidal category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})
can be viewed as a (symmetric) LDC
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\otimes,{\bf 1})
, when taking the tensor and par structures to be equal to the original monoidal structure, i.e.
\otimes=\invamp
and
{\bf 1}=\Bot
. In this case, the linear distributors are just the
\otimes
-associators:

\displaystyle\delta^{R}_{A,B,C}={\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A,B,C}\,\colon(A\otimes B)\otimes C\xrightarrow{\cong}A\otimes(B\otimes C)

\displaystyle\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}={\alpha^{-1}_{\otimes}}_{A,B,C}\,\colon A\otimes(B\otimes C)\xrightarrow{\cong}(A\otimes B)\otimes C

These LDCs are known as
degenerate
.

Degenerate LDCs are example of LDCs where the linear distributors are isomorphisms. In fact, LDCs whose linear distributors are isomorphisms are completely characterized by the categorical analogue of
shift monoids
. These are defined via invertible objects in a monoidal category, with which we can define a
shifted tensor
.

Definition 2.4

[
8
, Sec 5.2]
In a monoidal category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})
, an object
\Bot\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
is said to have a
\otimes
-inverse
if there is an object
-1
equipped with two isomorphisms
s^{L}\,\colon\Bot\otimes{}^{-1}\rightarrow{\bf 1}
and
s^{R}\,\colon{}^{-1}\otimes\Bot\rightarrow{\bf 1}
such that the following diagram commutes:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 28.3004pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-26.19704pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{({}^{-1}\otimes\Bot)\otimes{}^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.27527pt\raise 7.08289pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.3704pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{{}^{-1},\Bot,\Bot{-1}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 81.17192pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-28.3004pt\raise-9.80556pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.58269pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{s^{R}\otimes 1_{{}^{-1}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-11.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 50.68448pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 81.17192pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 107.36896pt\raise-9.80556pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.58269pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1_{{}^{-1}}\otimes s^{L}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 107.36896pt\raise-11.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-12.74712pt\raise-20.52216pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\bf 1}\otimes{}^{-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 16.56241pt\raise-40.11125pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.58269pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{u^{L}_{\otimes}}^{-1}_{{}^{-1}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.19704pt\raise-38.57352pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 50.68448pt\raise-20.52216pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 94.62184pt\raise-20.52216pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}^{-1}\otimes{\bf 71.39285pt\raise-40.11429pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.58269pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{u^{R}_{\otimes}}^{-1}_{{}^{-1}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 60.17194pt\raise-38.56717pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-41.04431pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 47.19704pt\raise-41.04431pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}^{-1}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(8)

If
\Bot
is an object in a monoidal category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})
with a
\otimes
-inverse, we can define a new monoidal structure
\invamp
on
{\mathbb{X}}
where
A\invamp B=A\otimes({}^{-1}\otimes B)
, called the
\Bot
-shifted tensor
, and where the monoidal unit is
\Bot
.

Proposition 2.5

[
6
, Prop 5.3 & 5.4]
Let
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})
be a monoidal category with an object
\Bot\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
with a
\otimes
-inverse. Then
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\invamp,\Bot)
, where
\invamp
is the
\Bot
-shifted tensor, is a LDC, known as a
shifted-tensor
LDC, with invertible linear distributors defined as follows:

\displaystyle\delta^{R}_{A,B,C}={\alpha_{\otimes}}_{A,{}^{-1}\otimes B,C};(1_{A}\otimes{\alpha_{\otimes}}_{{}^{-1},B,C})\,\colon(A\otimes({}^{-1}\otimes B))\otimes C\xrightarrow{\cong}A\otimes({}^{-1}\otimes(B\otimes C))

\displaystyle\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}={\alpha_{\otimes}^{-1}}_{A,B,{}^{-1}\otimes C}\,\colon A\otimes(B\otimes({}^{-1}\otimes C))\xrightarrow{\cong}(A\otimes B)\otimes({}^{-1}\otimes C)

Conversely, for every LDC with invertible linear distributors, the
\invamp
-unit
\Bot
has a
\otimes
-inverse,
{}^{-1}:={\bf 1}\invamp{\bf 1}
, and moreover
\invamp
is naturally equivalent to the
\Bot
-shifted tensor.

In particular, for any monoidal category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})
, the monoidal unit
{\bf 1}
is its own
\otimes
-inverse and the induced
{\bf 1}
-shifted tensor is (up to natural isomorphism) the starting monoidal product
\otimes
.

2.3
Mix

The categorical semantics of many variants of MLL can be expressed using (symmetric) LDCs. An important one of these variants is MLL with the MIX rule
A\otimes B\dashv A\invamp B
, which is equivalent to the nullary MIX rule
\Bot\dashv{\bf 1}
in the presence of the cut rule. As such, this amounts to asking that our LDC has a map from the
\invamp
-unit to the
\otimes
-unit.

Definition 2.6

[
7
, Def 6.2]

A LDC is
mix
if there is a map
m\,\colon\Bot\rightarrow{\bf 1}
, called the
nullary mix map
, such that the following diagram commutes:

62.98756pt\raise-56.77771pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\invamp({\bf 1}\otimes 123.14157pt\raise-63.34955pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.14684pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1_{A}\invamp{u^{L}_{\otimes}}_{B}^{-1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 161.62639pt\raise-56.77771pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 161.62639pt\raise-56.77771pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\invamp B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(9)

The induced natural transformation
\mathrm{mix}_{A,B}\,\colon A\otimes B\rightarrow A\invamp B
, defined by the equivalent composites above, is called the
mix map
.

The mix map is of course natural, as well as compatible with the associators and unitors (and symmetry) in the obvious way. For example, here is the compatibility between the mix map and the associators, which we will need later on.

(10)

The following is a useful lemma which greatly simplifies checking if a LDC is mix.

Lemma 2.8

[
7
, Lem 6.2]

A LDC is mix if and only if there is a map
m\,\colon\Bot\rightarrow{\bf 1}
for which (
9
) holds for any one of the following cases: (1)
A=B={\bf 1}
, (2)
A=B=\Bot
, (3)
A=\Bot
and
B={\bf 1}
, or (4)
A={\bf 1}
and
B=\Bot
.

There is a stronger version of the nullary MIX rule, which often holds in categorical models, that is
\Bot\dashv\vdash{\bf 1}
. In this case, we have that
\Bot\cong{\bf 1}
.

Definition 2.9

[
7
, Def 6.5]

A LDC is
isomix
if it is mix and the nullary mix map
{m\,\colon\Bot\rightarrow{\bf 1}}
is an isomorphism.

In fact, we do not need to check (
9
) if the two monoidal units are isomorphic.

Lemma 2.10

[
7
, Lem 6.6]

A LDC for which there is an isomorphism
{\bf 1}\cong\Bot
is isomix.

It is important to stress that in an isomix LDC, even if
{\bf 1}\cong\Bot
, the induced mix map
\mathrm{mix}_{A,B}\,\colon A\otimes B\rightarrow A\invamp B
is not necessarily an isomorphism.

Definition 2.11

[
10
, Sec 2.3]

An LDC is
compact
if it is isomix and the mix maps
\mathrm{mix}_{A,B}\,\colon A\otimes B\rightarrow A\invamp B
are isomorphisms.

Of course, every degenerate LDC is a compact LDC, where the mix maps are identities. Moreover, by Lemma
2.7
, the linear distributors of a compact LDC are essentially the associators (modulo the mix maps). In particular, a compact LDC has invertible linear distributors, and thus we can further equivalently characterize compact LDCs as the isomix LDCs with invertible distributors.

2.4
Complements and Negation

The most important class of LDCs are arguably
\ast
-autonomous categories
. However, unlike LDCs,
\ast
-autonomous categories make the multiplicative conjunction and negation primitive, then define the multiplicative disjunctions by de Morgan duality.

Example 2.12

Briefly recall that a
\ast
-autonomous category can be defined as a symmetric monoidal category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})
equipped with a full and faithful contravariant functor
\ast
such that there are natural isomorphisms
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}(A\otimes B,C^{\ast})\cong\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}(A,(B\otimes C)^{\ast})
. Then every
\ast
-autonomous category is a SLDC, with the tensor structure given by the original monoidal structure and the par structure given by de Morgan duality,
A\invamp B=(B^{\ast}\otimes A^{\ast})^{\ast}
and
\Bot={\bf 1}^{\ast}
. Some notable examples of
\ast
-autonomous categories include the category of finite-dimensional vector spaces, the category of sup-lattices, the category of coherence spaces and the category of finiteness spaces.

In fact
\ast
-autonomous categories are precisely the SLDCs with
negation
. However, before reviewing negation, we take a slight detour and talk about complemented objects (which we will also need for the results in Sec
3.2
).

Definition 2.13

[
9
, Def A.5]

In a LDC, a
complementation pair
is a triple
(A,A^{c},\gamma,\tau)
consisting of an object
A
, called the
left complement
, an object
A^{c}
, called the
right complement
, and maps
\gamma\,\colon A\otimes A^{c}\rightarrow\Bot
, called the
evaluation
, and
\tau\,\colon{\bf 1}\rightarrow A^{c}\invamp A
, called the
coevaluation
, such that the following diagrams commute:

272.49792pt\raise-13.18057pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.88434pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{u^{R}_{\invamp}}_{A^{c}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 272.49792pt\raise-5.36113pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 59.06808pt\raise-56.4564pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 89.16394pt\raise-42.20166pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\gamma\invamp 1_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 241.41855pt\raise-56.4564pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 241.41855pt\raise-56.4564pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A^{c}\invamp(A\otimes 272.49792pt\raise-42.20166pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.575pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1_{A^{c}}\invamp\gamma}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 272.49792pt\raise-35.33577pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(11)

In any LDC, the
\otimes
-unit and the
\invamp
-unit always form a complementation pair.

Proposition 2.14

[
9
, Prop A.7]

In a LDC,
(\Bot,{\bf 1},{u^{R}_{\invamp}}^{-1}_{\bf 1}\,\colon{\bf 1}\rightarrow{\bf 1}\invamp\Bot,{u^{R}_{\otimes}}^{-1}\,\colon\Bot\otimes{\bf 1}\rightarrow\Bot)
is a complementation pair.

As one might expect, complementation pairs induce adjunctions.

Lemma 2.15

[
8
, Lem 4.4]

Let
(A,A^{c},\gamma,\tau)
be a complementation pair in a LDC
{\mathbb{X}}
. Then there is an adjunction
A\otimes-\dashv A^{c}\invamp-
, in other words there is a natural isomorphism between the hom-sets
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}(A\otimes B,C)\cong\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}(B,A^{c}\invamp C)
.

As such, for a complementation pair
(A,A^{c},\gamma,\tau)
, we may see
A^{c}
as essentially the “right negation” of
A
, and dually that
A
is the “left negation” of
A^{c}
. Thus we say that a LDC has negation if there is a coherent way of associating to every object both a left negation and a right negation. For a SLDC, we ask that the left negation and right negation be the same.

Definition 2.16

[
8
, Def 4.1, 4.3]

A SLDC has
negation
if there is an object function
(-)^{\perp}
, together with the following parametrized family of maps

\displaystyle\gamma_{A}^{R}\,\colon A\otimes A^{\perp}\rightarrow\bot

\displaystyle\tau^{R}_{A}\,\colon\top\rightarrow A\oplus A^{\perp}

which additionally induce the following families,

\gamma_{A}^{L}=A^{\perp}\otimes A\xrightarrow{{\sigma_{\otimes}}_{A^{\perp},A}}A\otimes A^{\perp}\xrightarrow{\gamma^{R}_{A}}\bot\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\tau^{L}_{A}=\top\xrightarrow{\tau^{R}_{A}}A\oplus A^{\perp}\xrightarrow{{\sigma_{\oplus}}_{A,A^{\perp}}}A^{\perp}\oplus A

such
(A,A^{\perp},\gamma^{R}_{A},\tau^{L}_{A})
and
(A^{\perp},A,\gamma^{L}_{A},\tau^{R}_{A})
form complementation pairs.

Theorem 2.17

[
8
, Thm 4.5]

The notions of SLDCs with negation and
\ast
-autonomous categories coincide.

2.5
Linear Functors

We conclude this background section by reviewing the appropriate notion of morphisms between LDCs. The definition was determined in order to develop the appropriate version of exponential modalities for LDCs. We do note review the full definition here, as we will not necessarily need to know all the axioms for the story of this paper, and instead invite the curious reader to see
[
9
]
for full details.

Definition 2.18

[
9
, Def 1]
A
(bilax) linear functor

F=(F_{\otimes},F_{\invamp})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
between LDCs consists of:

(i)

A (lax)
\otimes
-monoidal functor
(F_{\otimes},m_{\bf 1},m_{\otimes})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})
, with underlying functor
{F_{\otimes}:\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\to\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}}
, unit isomorphism (resp. map)
m_{\bf 1}\,\colon{\bf 1}\rightarrow F_{\otimes}({\bf 1})
, and natural monoidal isomorphism (resp. transformation)

{{m_{\otimes}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A)\otimes F_{\otimes}(B)\rightarrow F_{\otimes}(A\otimes B)}
;

(ii)

A (colax)
\invamp
-monoidal functor
(F_{\invamp},n,n_{\invamp})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\invamp,\Bot)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\invamp,\Bot)
, with underlying functor
F_{\invamp}:\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\to\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
, unit isomorphism (resp. map)
n\,\colon F_{\invamp}(\Bot)\rightarrow\Bot
, and natural comonoidal isomorphism (resp. transformation)
{n_{\invamp}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\invamp}(A\invamp B)\rightarrow F_{\invamp}(A)\invamp F_{\invamp}(B)
;

equipped with four natural transformations, known as the
linear strengths
,

\displaystyle{v_{\otimes}^{R}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A\invamp B)\rightarrow F_{\invamp}(A)\invamp F_{\otimes}(B)

\displaystyle{v_{\otimes}^{L}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A\invamp B)\rightarrow F_{\otimes}(A)\invamp F_{\invamp}(B)

\displaystyle{v_{\invamp}^{R}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\otimes}(A)\otimes F_{\invamp}(B)\rightarrow F_{\invamp}(A\otimes B)

\displaystyle{v_{\invamp}^{L}}_{A,B}\,\colon F_{\invamp}(A)\otimes F_{\otimes}(B)\rightarrow F_{\invamp}(A\otimes B)

such that various coherence conditions detailed in
[
9
]
hold.

We shall also be particularly interested in restricting our attention to linear functors whose component functors are in fact equal. These were first defined in
[
4
]
under the name degenerate linear functors. They were renamed
Frobenius
linear functors in
[
10
]
as they generalize Frobenius monoidal functors. We shall use this latter terminology.

Definition 2.19

[
10
, Def 3.1]

A
(bilax) Frobenius
linear functor
F=(F_{\otimes},F_{\invamp})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
is a (bilax) linear functor such that
F_{\otimes}=F_{\invamp}
,
{\nu^{R}_{\otimes}}_{A,B}={\nu^{L}_{\otimes}}_{A,B}={n_{\invamp}}_{A,B}
, and
{\nu^{R}_{\invamp}}_{A,B}={\nu^{L}_{\invamp}}_{A,B}={m_{\otimes}}_{A,B}
.

Given the degeneracy, we can give an alternative characterization of Frobenius linear functors.

Proposition 2.20

[
10
, Lem 3.2]
For LDCs
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}
, the following notions coincide:

(i)

A (bilax) Frobenius linear functor
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
;

(ii)

A functor
F:\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\to\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
which is a (lax)
\otimes
-monoidal functor
(F,m_{\bf 1},m_{\otimes})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})
and a (colax)
\invamp
-monoidal functor
(F,n,n_{\invamp})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\invamp,\Bot)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\invamp,\Bot)
such that the following diagrams commute:

(12)

So from now on we will denote Frobenius linear functors simply as
F\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\to\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
. If the LDCs are mix, the definition of Frobenius linear functors can be slightly extended to guarantee that they preserve the mix maps.

Definition 2.21

[
10
, Def 3.4]
For mix LDCs
{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\mathbb{Y}}
, a (bilax) Frobenius linear functor
F\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
is mix if the following diagram commutes:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern (13)

Proposition 2.22

[
10
, Lem 3.5]

A mix (bilax) Frobenius linear functor
F\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
preserves the mix maps, that is, following diagram commutes:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern (14)

With the introduction of linear functors, we can be more precise about the relationship between the different examples and classes of LDCs previously introduced. The following results are implicit in the work of Cockett and Seely, but have yet to explicitly appear in the literature, so we record them here.

Proposition 2.23

Let
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\invamp,\Bot)
be a compact LDC. Then the
\otimes
-monoidal structure is isomorphic to the
\invamp
-monoidal structure,
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})\cong(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\invamp,\Bot)
. Moreover, it is isomorphic (as a LDC) to the degenerate LDC induced by the
\otimes
-monoidal structure and similarly induced by the
\invamp
-monoidal structure,
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\invamp,\Bot)\cong(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\otimes,{\bf 1})\cong(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\invamp,\Bot,\invamp,\Bot)
.

Proof:

Given a compact LDC
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\invamp,\Bot)
, the identity functor
1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}}\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
becomes a monoidal functor in four ways. Firstly and secondly, we have the identity monoidal functors
(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}},1_{\bf 1},1_{A\otimes B})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})
and
(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}},1,1_{A\invamp B})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\invamp,\Bot)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\invamp,\Bot)
. Thirdly and fourthly,
(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}},m,\mathrm{mix}^{-1}_{A,B})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\invamp,\Bot)
and
(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}},m^{-1},\mathrm{mix}_{A,B})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\invamp,\Bot)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})
are also monoidal functors. These latter two are inverses which mediate the isomorphism
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1})\cong(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\invamp,\Bot)
.

Furthermore, pairing the first and last monoidal functor structures determines a Frobenius linear functor

(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}},1_{\bf 1},1_{A\otimes B},m^{-1},\mathrm{mix}_{A,B})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\otimes,{\bf 1})\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\invamp,\Bot)
. Indeed, the Frobenius linear functor coherence diagrams (
12
) simply become the top squares of (
10
), which commute in any mix LDC. Similarly, pairing the first and third structures determines its inverse
(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}},1_{\bf 1},1_{A\otimes B},m,\mathrm{mix}^{-1}_{A,B})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\invamp,\Bot)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\otimes,{\bf 1})
, mediating the isomorphism
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\invamp,\Bot)\cong(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\otimes,{\bf 1})
. Pairing the second monoidal functor with the third and then the fourth provides the isomorphism
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\invamp,\Bot)\cong(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\invamp,\Bot,\invamp,\Bot)
.

\Box

Proposition 2.24

Let
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\invamp,\Bot)
be a LDC with invertible linear distributors. Then we have that the
\invamp
-monoidal structure is isomorphic to the
\Bot
-shifted tensor structure,
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\invamp,\Bot)\cong(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\cdot\otimes(({\bf 1}\invamp{\bf 1})\otimes\cdot),\Bot)
. Moreover, it is isomorphic (as a LDC) to the
\Bot
-shifted LDC,
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\invamp,\Bot)\cong(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\cdot\otimes(({\bf 1}\invamp{\bf 1})\otimes\cdot),\Bot)
.

Proof:

By Proposition
2.5
,
\Bot
has a
\otimes
-inverse given by
{\bf 1}\invamp{\bf 1}
and
\cdot\otimes(({\bf 1}\invamp{\bf 1})\otimes\cdot)
is a well-defined monoidal product with unit
\Bot
. Now, define
\beta_{A,B}
to be the following natural isomorphism

A\otimes(({\bf 1}\invamp{\bf 1})\otimes B)\xrightarrow{1_{A}\otimes\delta^{R}_{{\bf 1},{\bf 1},B}}A\otimes({\bf 1}\invamp(1\otimes B))\xrightarrow{\delta^{L}_{A,{\bf 1},{\bf 1}\otimes B}}(A\otimes{\bf 1})\invamp({\bf 1}\otimes B)\xrightarrow{{u^{R}_{\otimes}}^{-1}_{A}\invamp{u^{L}_{\otimes}}^{-1}_{B}}A\invamp B

Then,
(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}},1,\beta_{A,B})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\invamp,\Bot)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\cdot\otimes(({\bf 1}\invamp{\bf 1})\otimes\cdot),\Bot)
is a monoidal functor with inverse
(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}},1,\beta^{-1}_{A,B})
, mediating the desired isomorphism between monoidal categories.

Pairing the identity
\otimes
-monoidal functor with the above structure determines a Frobenius linear functor

(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}},1,1_{A\otimes B},1,\beta^{-1}_{A,B})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\invamp,\Bot)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\cdot\otimes(({\bf 1}\invamp{\bf 1})\otimes\cdot),\Bot)
. The coherence conditions (
12
) follow from a straightforward diagram chase. Similarly, it has an inverse
(1_{\mbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{X}}$}},1,1_{A\otimes B},1,\beta_{A,B})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\cdot\otimes(({\bf 1}\invamp{\bf 1})\otimes\cdot),\Bot)\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\otimes,{\bf 1},\invamp,\Bot)
, providing the desired isomorphism of LDCs.

\Box

3
Cartesian Linearly Distributive Categories

In this section, we discuss cartesian linearly distributive categories (CLDC), which are of course the principal notion of study of this paper. After reviewing their definition, we will revisit how a CLDC is in fact mix, and also provide our initial novel observations about CLDCs, such as their duoidal structure and that they have a preinitial-subterminal coincidence.

By a
cartesian category
we mean a category with finite products, where we denote the binary product by
\times
and the terminal object by
\top
. Projections maps are denoted as
{\pi^{0}_{A,B}:A\times B\to A}
and
\pi^{1}_{A,B}:A\times B\to B
, with pairing operation
\langle-,-\rangle
, and we denote the unique map to the terminal object by
t_{A}:A\to\top
. Dually, a
cocartesian category
is a category with finite coproducts, where we denote the binary coproduct by
+
and the initial object by
\bot
. Injections maps are denoted as
{\iota^{0}_{A,B}:A\to A+B}
and
\iota^{1}_{A,B}:B\to A+B
, with pairing operation
[-,-]
, and we denote the unique map from the initial object by
b_{A}:\bot\to A
. A (co)cartesian category
{\mathbb{X}}
canonically becomes a symmetric monoidal category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\times,\top)
(resp.
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},+,\bot)
). We say that a symmetric monoidal category is (co)cartesian if its monoidal product is a (co)product and its monoidal unit is a (resp. initial) terminal object.

3.1
Cartesian Linearly Distributive Categories

We can now introduce the main definition of interest:

Definition 3.1

[
8
, Sec 2]

A
cartesian linearly distributive category
(CLDC)
{\mathbb{X}}
is a SLDC whose tensor monoidal structure is cartesian and whose par monoidal structure is cocartesian.

So for a CLDC, its tensor is a product
\times
, its tensor unit is a terminal object
\top
, its par is a coproduct
+
, and its par unit an initial object
\bot
, and its linear distributors are of type:

\displaystyle\delta^{R}_{A,B,C}\,\colon(A+B)\times C\rightarrow A+(B\times C)

\displaystyle\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}\,\colon A\times(B+C)\rightarrow(A\times B)+C.

While it is a straightforward and natural concept to consider, we shall see that this gives rise to a highly peculiar form of distributivity, leading to surprisingly strong properties and results. We refrain from giving examples in this section, as that will be a key topic discussed in Sec
4
,
5
and
7
. We begin our investigation of CLDCs with a useful result concerning the interaction between the linear distributors and certain projections and injections.

Lemma 3.2

In a CLDC, the following equations hold:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\delta^{L}_{A,B,C};(\pi^{1}_{A,B}+1_{C})=\pi^{1}_{A,B+C}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt(1_{A}\times\iota^{0}_{B,C});\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}=\iota^{0}_{A\times B,C}\\
&\delta^{R}_{A,B,C};(1_{A}+\pi^{0}_{B,C})=\pi^{0}_{A+B,C}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt(\iota^{1}_{A,B}\times 1_{C});\delta^{R}_{A,B,C}=\iota^{1}_{A,B\times C}\end{split}

(15)

Proof:
The first equation follows from the fact that in a cartesian category,
\pi^{1}=(t\times 1);{u_{\times}^{L}}^{-1}
, and using the unit coherence of the linear distributor (
4
):

\displaystyle\delta^{L}_{A,B,C};(\pi^{1}_{A,B}+1_{C})=\delta^{L}_{A,B,C};((t_{A}\times 1_{B})+1_{C});({u^{L}_{\times}}^{-1}_{B}+1_{C})

\displaystyle=(t_{A}\times 1_{B+C});\delta^{L}_{\top,B,C};({u^{L}_{\times}}^{-1}_{B}+1_{C})=(t_{A}\times 1_{B+C});{u_{\times}^{L}}^{-1}_{B+C}=\pi^{1}_{A.B+C}

The other equations are proved in similar (dual) fashion.

\Box

The appropriate definition of morphism between CLDCs is one that preserves products and coproducts, which from a LDC perspective simply amounts to asking for linear functors (since asking that a functor preserves (co)products is equivalent to asking it be monoidal). We will also be particularly interested in restricting our attention to Frobenius cartesian linear functors.

Definition 3.3

A
(Frobenius) cartesian linear functor
between CLDCs is a (Frobenius) linear functor between the CLDCs.

We denote the category of CLDCs and (Frobenius) cartesian linear functors between them by
\mathbf{CLDC}
(resp.
\mathbf{FCLDC}
).

3.2
Subterminal Initial and Preinitial Terminal

Recall that in a category, an object
A
is
preinitial
if there is at most one map from
A
to another object, and dually
subterminal object
if there is at most one map from another object to
A
. It turns out that in a CLDC, the initial object is always
subterminal
and the terminal object is always
preinitial
. This is quite a strong property which highlights the uniqueness of CLDCs and also helps quickly check if a category is not a CLDC. Here are first some useful equivalent characterizations of being subterminal (resp. preinitial) in (co)cartesian categories.

Lemma 3.4

[
5
, Lem 3.2]
In a cartesian category, the following are equivalent:

(i)

A
is subterminal,

(ii)

\Delta_{A}=\langle 1_{A},1_{A}\rangle\,\colon A\rightarrow A\times A
is an isomorphism,

(iii)

\pi^{0}_{A,A}=\pi^{1}_{A,A}\,\colon A\times A\rightarrow A
.

Dually, in a cocartesian category, the following are equivalent:

(i)

A
is preinitial,

(ii)

\nabla_{A}=[1_{A},1_{A}]\,\colon A+A\rightarrow A
is an isomorphism,

(iii)

\iota^{0}_{A,A}=\iota^{1}_{A,A}\,\colon A\rightarrow A+A
.

Proposition 3.5

In a CLDC, the initial object
\bot
is subterminal and the terminal object
\top
is preinitial.

Proof:

First recall that by Proposition
2.14
,
\bot
and
\top
form a complementation pair. Then by Lemma
2.15
,
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}(\bot\times\bot,\bot)\cong\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}(\bot,\top+\bot)
. As
\bot
is the initial object, there is a unique map of type
\bot\to\top+\bot
, and therefore there is unique map of type
\bot\times\bot\to\bot
. As such, the projections
\pi^{0}_{\bot,\bot}
and
\pi^{1}_{\bot,\bot}
must be equal. Then by Lemma
3.4
,
\bot
is subterminal. Similarly, by Lemma
2.15
, we also have that
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}(\top,\top+\top)\cong\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}(\bot\times\top,\top)
. As
\top
is the terminal object, there is a unique map of type
\bot\times\top\to\top
, and thus there is a unique map of type
\top\to\top+\top
. So the injections
\iota^{0}_{\top,\top}
and
\iota^{1}_{\top,\top}
must be equal. So by Lemma
3.4
again,
\top
is preinitial.

\Box

Since
\bot
is subterminal and
\top
is preinitial, we can ask for which objects
A
is there a map
A\to\bot
or
\top\to A
. This is precisely the case when
A\cong A\times\bot
or
A\cong A+\top
respectively.

Lemma 3.6

Let
A
be an object in a CLDC. Then:

(i)

There exists a map
A\rightarrow\bot
if and only if
\pi^{0}_{A,\bot}\,\colon A\times\bot\rightarrow A
is an isomorphism;

(ii)

There exists a map
\top\rightarrow A
if and only if
\iota^{0}_{A,\top}\,\colon A\rightarrow A+\top
is an isomorphism.

Proof:
We prove
(i)
. For the
\Rightarrow
direction, suppose that
\pi^{0}_{A,\bot}\,\colon A\times\bot\rightarrow A
is an isomorphism, so
A\cong A\times\bot
. Then we can construct the map
A\cong A\times\bot\xrightarrow{\pi^{1}_{A,\bot}}\bot
as desired. For the
\Leftarrow
direction, suppose there exists a map
a\,\colon A\rightarrow\bot
. Then consider the map
\langle 1_{A},a\rangle\,\colon A\rightarrow A\times\bot
. By definition, we have that
\langle 1_{A},a\rangle;\pi^{0}_{A,\bot}=1_{A}
. On the other hand, we obviously have that
\pi^{0}_{A,\bot};\langle 1_{A},a\rangle;\pi^{0}_{A,\bot}=\pi^{0}_{A,\bot}
, while we can also compute that:

\pi^{0}_{A,\bot};\langle 1_{A},a\rangle;\pi^{1}_{A,\bot}=\pi^{0}_{A,\bot};a=\pi^{1}_{A,\bot}

where the lastly equality holds since there is a unique map
A\times\bot\rightarrow\bot
as
\bot
is subterminal. Then by the universal property of the product we get that
\pi^{0}_{A,\bot};\langle 1_{A},a\rangle=1_{A\times\bot}
. Thus
\pi^{0}_{A,\bot}
is an isomorphism with inverse
\langle 1_{A},a\rangle
. By similar arguments, one can also show
(ii)
.

\Box

3.3
Mix Structure

In a CLDC, there is a unique map from the initial object to the terminal object, using the universal property of either object. In fact, this unique map is a nullary mix map, meaning that a CLDC is mix. This fact was first proved by Cockett and Seely in
[
7
]
. However the proof they give is somewhat involved as it works with the
nucleus
(the subcategory of complemented objects) and then applies Joyal’s paradox (which we review in Sec
4.2
) to conclude that a CLDC must be mix. Here we give an alternative streamlined version of their proof, utilizing the same core ideas and making use of the fact that the initial object is subterminal.

Proposition 3.7

[
7
, Rem 5.4]

A CLDC is mix with
m=b_{\top}=t_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
.

Proof:

By Lemma
2.8
, it suffices to prove (
9
) for
\bot\times\bot\rightarrow\bot+\bot
, which holds by commutativity of the following diagram:

\textstyle{\bot\times\bot\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{\bot}\times{u_{+}}^{-1}_{\bot}}
\scriptstyle{{u_{+}}^{-1}_{\bot}\times 1_{\bot}}
\textstyle{\bot\times(\bot+\bot)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{\bot}\times(m+1_{\bot})}
\scriptstyle{1_{\bot}\times{u_{+}}_{\bot}}
\scriptstyle{({\rm nat})}
\textstyle{\bot\times(\top+\bot)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{L}_{\bot,\top,\bot}}
(
4
)
\scriptstyle{1_{\bot}\times{u_{+}^{R}}_{\top}}
\textstyle{(\bot+\bot)\times\bot\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{({\rm nat})}
\scriptstyle{(1_{\bot}+m)\times 1_{\bot}}
\scriptstyle{{u_{+}}_{\bot}\times 1_{\bot}}
\textstyle{\bot\times\bot\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{\bot}\times m}
\scriptstyle{m\times 1_{\bot}}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{0}_{\bot,\bot}}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{1}_{\bot,\bot}}
\textstyle{\bot\times\top\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{({\rm nat})}
\scriptstyle{{u^{R}_{+}}_{\bot\times\top}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{{u^{R}_{\times}}^{-1}_{\bot}}
\textstyle{(\bot\times\bot)+\bot\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{u^{R}_{\times}}^{-1}_{\bot}+1_{\bot}}
\textstyle{\top\times\bot\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{u^{L}_{+}}^{-1}_{\top\times\bot}}
(
4
)
\scriptstyle{{u^{L}_{\times}}^{-1}_{\bot}}
\textstyle{\bot\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{u_{+}}^{-1}_{\bot}}
\textstyle{(\bot+\top)\times\bot\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{u^{L}_{+}}_{\top}\times 1_{\bot}}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{R}_{\bot,\top,\bot}}
\textstyle{\bot+(\top\times\bot)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{\bot}+{u_{\times}^{L}}_{\bot}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{({\rm nat})}
\textstyle{\bot+\bot}

\Box

Therefore, for all pairs of objects
A
and
B
in a CLDC, there exists a mix map from their product to their coproduct:
\mathrm{mix}_{A,B}\,\colon A\times B\rightarrow A+B
.

Lemma 3.8

A cartesian Frobenius linear functor is a mix Frobenius linear functor.

Proof:

By definition of a cartesian linear functor,
n_{\bot}
and
m_{\top}
are isomorphisms. Then, as
\bot
is initial (or
\top
is terminal), we get
m=n_{\bot}^{-1};F(m);m_{\top}^{-1}
, meaning (
13
) holds.

\Box

3.4
Preinitial and Subterminal Coincidence

We now wish to show that in a CLDC, being preinitial is equivalent to being subterminal. To do so, let us first give an equivalent characterization of being preinitial or subterminal using the mix maps.

Lemma 3.9

Let
A
be an object in a CLDC. Then:

(i)

A
is preinitial if and only if the following equalities hold:

\displaystyle\Delta_{A};\mathrm{mix}_{A,A};\nabla_{A}=1_{A}

\displaystyle\nabla_{A};\Delta_{A};\mathrm{mix}_{A,A}=1_{A+A}

(16)

(ii)

A
is subterminal if and only if the following equations hold.

\displaystyle\Delta_{A};\mathrm{mix}_{A,A};\nabla_{A}=1_{A}

\displaystyle\mathrm{mix}_{A,A};\nabla_{A};\Delta_{A}=1_{A\times A}

(17)

Proof:
We prove
(i)
. For the
\Leftarrow
direction, suppose that
A
is preinitial. Then there is a unique map of type
A\to A
, and thus
\Delta_{A};m_{A,A};\nabla_{A}=1_{A}
. On the other hand, by Lemma
3.4
,
\nabla_{A}^{-1}
exists, so we compute:

\nabla_{A};\Delta_{A};\mathrm{mix}_{A,A}=\nabla_{A};\Delta_{A};\mathrm{mix}_{A,A};\nabla_{A};\nabla_{A}^{-1}=\nabla_{A};1_{A};\nabla_{A}^{-1}=1_{A+A}

So (
16
) holds as desired. For the
\Rightarrow
direction, suppose that (
16
) holds. Observe that this says that
\nabla_{A}
is an isomorphism with inverse
\Delta_{A};\mathrm{mix}_{A,A}\,\colon A\rightarrow A+A
. So by Lemma
3.4
,
A
is preinitial. By similar arguments, one can also show
(ii)
.

\Box

Using the above lemma, we can prove the desired equivalence between being preinitial and being subterminal in a CLDC. In fact, we do so by showing that for a preinitial or subterminal object
A
, the mix map
A\times A\to A+A
must be of a specific form.

Proposition 3.10

Let
A
be an object in a CLDC. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

A
is preinitial;

(ii)

A
is subterminal;

(iii)

The following diagram commutes:

By definition, the upper composites of the above diagrams are the two equivalent definitions of
\mathrm{mix}_{A,A}
. For
(iii)\Rightarrow(i)
, suppose (
18
) holds. Then we first compute that:

\Delta_{A};\mathrm{mix}_{A,A};\nabla_{A}=\Delta_{A};\pi^{j}_{A,A};\iota^{j}_{A,A};\nabla_{A}=1_{A};1_{A}=1_{A}

We can also compute that:

\iota^{0}_{A,A};\nabla_{A};\Delta_{A};\mathrm{mix}_{A,A}=\iota^{0}_{A,A};\nabla_{A};\Delta_{A};\pi^{0}_{A,A};\iota^{0}_{A,A}=1_{A};1_{A};\iota^{0}_{A,A}=\iota^{0}_{A,A}

\iota^{1}_{A,A};\nabla_{A};\Delta_{A};\mathrm{mix}_{A,A}=\iota^{1}_{A,A};\nabla_{A};\Delta_{A};\pi^{1}_{A,A};\iota^{1}_{A,A}

Then by the universal property of the coproduct,
\nabla_{A};\Delta_{A};\mathrm{mix}_{A,A}=1_{A+A}
. Then by Lemma
3.9
,
A
is preinitial.

We now show
(ii)\Leftrightarrow(iii)
. For
(ii)\Rightarrow(iii)
, suppose that
A
is subterminal, then the following diagrams commute

3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(A\times\top)+A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 175.81326pt\raise-42.86874pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.60004pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.14684pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{u^{R}_{\times}}^{-1}_{A}+1_{A}}$}}}\kern 3.60004pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 175.81326pt\raise-49.7375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-6.46802pt\raise-56.575pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 23.38617pt\raise-36.74355pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.60004pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.14684pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{u^{L}_{+}}_{A}^{-1}}$}}}\kern 3.60004pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 67.30676pt\raise-33.4729pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.02695pt\raise-63.90938pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.60004pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.80934pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota^{1}_{A,A}}$}}}\kern 3.60004pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 160.22447pt\raise-56.575pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 78.99695pt\raise-56.575pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 160.22447pt\raise-56.575pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A+A}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

Observe that the upper composites are two other equivalent definitions of
m_{A,A}
(by naturality of the linear distributors). For
(iii)\Rightarrow(ii)
, suppose that (
18
) holds. Then above we already showed that
\Delta_{A};\mathrm{mix}_{A,A};\nabla_{A}=1_{A}
. We can also compute that:

\mathrm{mix}_{A,A};\nabla_{A};\Delta_{A};\pi^{0}_{A,A}=\pi^{0}_{A,A};\iota^{0}_{A,A};\nabla_{A};\Delta_{A};\pi^{0}_{A,A}=\pi^{0}_{A,A};1_{A};1_{A}=\pi^{0}_{A,A}

\mathrm{mix}_{A,A};\nabla_{A};\Delta_{A};\pi^{1}_{A,A}=\pi^{1}_{A,A};\iota^{1}_{A,A};\nabla_{A};\Delta_{A};\pi^{1}_{A,A}=\pi^{1}_{A,A};1_{A};1_{A}=\pi^{1}_{A,A}

Then by the universal property of the product,
\mathrm{mix}_{A,A};\nabla_{A};\Delta_{A}=1_{A\times A}
. Then by Lemma
3.9
,
A
is subterminal.

Putting all this together we get
(i)\Leftrightarrow(iiii)\Leftrightarrow(ii)
. So we conclude that
A
is preinitial if only if
A
is subterminal, as desired.

\Box

3.5
Duoidal Structure

As discussed in the introduction, duoidal categories are another important type of category with two monoidal structures that have a some sort special distributivity. Here we explain how every CLDC is duoidal and study its duoidal structure. For an in-depth introduction to duoidal categories, we invite the reader to see
[
1
]
.

Definition 3.11

[
1
, Def 6.1]

A
duoidal category

(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\diamond,I,\star,J)
is category
{\mathbb{X}}
with two monoidal structures
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\diamond,I)
and
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\star,J)
, and equipped with morphisms

\displaystyle\Delta_{I}

\displaystyle\,\colon I\rightarrow I\star I

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\nabla_{J}

\displaystyle\,\colon J\diamond J\rightarrow J

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988ptm

\displaystyle\,\colon I\rightarrow J

and a natural transformation:

\displaystyle\mu_{A,B,C,D}\,\colon(A\star B)\diamond(C\star D)\rightarrow(A\diamond C)\star(B\diamond D)

called the
interchange
, such that various coherence conditions detailed in
[
1
]
hold.

Duoidal structures arise canonically whenever monoidal categories have finite products or finite coproduces. Therefore, every category which has both finite products and finite coproducts is a duoidal category, as detailed in
[
1
, Ex 6.19]
. Explicitly, the structure maps
\Delta_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\bot\times\bot
,
\nabla_{\top}\,\colon\top+\top\rightarrow\top
, and
m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
are defined respectively as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta_{\bot}=b_{\bot\times\bot}=\langle 1_{\bot},1_{\bot}\rangle

\displaystyle\nabla_{\top}=t_{\top+\top}=[1_{\top},1_{\top}]

\displaystyle m=t_{\bot}=b_{\top}

(19)

while the interchange
\mu_{A,B,C,D}\,\colon(A\times B)+(C\times D)\rightarrow(A+C)\times(B+D)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mu_{A,B,C,D}

\displaystyle=\langle\pi^{0}_{A,B}+\pi^{0}_{C,D},\pi^{1}_{A,B}+\pi^{1}_{C,D}\rangle=[\iota^{0}_{A,C}\times\iota^{0}_{B,D},\iota^{1}_{A,C}\times\iota^{1}_{B,D}]

(20)

Therefore, since every category which is cartesian and cocartesian is duoidal, we get that every CLDC is duoidal, and moreover the linear distributors are compatible with the interchange.

Proposition 3.12

A CLDC is a duoidal category with the duoidal structure given as in (
19
) and (
20
). Moreover, the linear distributors are compatible with the interchange in the sense that the following equalities hold:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(1_{X}\times\mu_{A,B,C,D});{\alpha_{\times}}^{-1}_{X,A+C,B+D};(\delta^{L}_{X,A,C}\times 1_{B+D})\\
&=\delta^{L}_{X,A\times B,C\times D};({\alpha_{\times}}^{-1}_{X,A,B}+1_{C\times D});\mu_{X\times A,B,C,D}\\
\\
&(1_{A\times B}+\delta^{L}_{C,D,X});{\alpha_{+}}_{A\times B,C\times D,X};(\mu_{A,B,C,D}+1_{X})\\
&=\mu_{A,B,C,D+X};(1_{A+C}\times{\alpha_{+}}_{B,D,X});\delta^{L}_{A+C,B+D,X}\\
\\
&(\mu_{A,B,C,D}\times 1_{X});{\alpha_{\times}}_{A+C,B+D,X};(1_{A+C}\times\delta^{R}_{B,D,X})\\
&=\delta^{R}_{A\times B,C\times D,X};(1_{A\times B}+{\alpha_{\times}}_{C,D,X});\mu_{A,B,C,D\times X}\\
\\
&(\delta^{R}_{X,A,B}+1_{C\times D});{\alpha_{+}}^{-1}_{X,A\times B,C\times D};(1_{X}+\mu_{A,B,C,D})\\
&=\mu_{X+A,B,C,D};({\alpha_{+}}^{-1}_{X,A,C}\times 1_{B+D});\delta^{R}_{X,A+C,B+D}\\
\end{split}

(21)

Proof:
As a commutative diagram, the first equality of (
21
) is:

\textstyle{X\times((A\times B)+(C\times D))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{L}_{X,A\times B,C\times D}}
\scriptstyle{1_{X}\times\mu_{A,B,C,D}}
\textstyle{(X\times(A\times B))+(C\times D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\alpha_{\times}}^{-1}_{X,A,B}+1_{C\times D}}
\textstyle{X\times((A+C)\times(B+D))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\alpha_{\times}}^{-1}_{X,A+C,B+D}}
\textstyle{((X\times A)\times B)+(C\times D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{X\times A,B,C,D}}
\textstyle{(X\times(A+C))\times(B+D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{L}_{X,A,C}\times 1_{B+D}}
\textstyle{((X\times A)+C)\times(B+D)}

We leave it as an exercise for the reader to draw out the other commutative diagrams.

Now the first equality of (
21
) holds by the following computation using the cartesian tensor structure:

\displaystyle\delta^{L}_{X,A\times B,C\times D};({\alpha_{\times}^{-1}}_{X,A,B}+1_{C\times D});\mu_{X\times A,B,C,D}

\displaystyle=\langle\delta^{L}_{X,A\times B,C\times D};({\alpha_{\times}^{-1}}_{X,A,B}+1_{C\times D});(\pi^{0}_{A,B}+\pi^{0}_{C,D}),\delta^{L}_{X,A\times B,C\times D};

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt({\alpha_{\times}^{-1}}_{X,A,B}+1_{C\times D});(\pi^{1}_{A,B}+\pi^{1}_{C,D})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\delta^{L}_{X,A\times B,C\times D};((1_{X}\times\pi^{0}_{A,B})+\pi^{0}_{C,D}),\delta^{L}_{X,A\times B,C\times D};(\pi^{1}_{X,A\times B}+1_{C\times D});(\pi^{1}_{A,B}+\pi^{1}_{C,D})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle(1_{X}\times(\pi^{0}_{A,B}+\pi^{0}_{C,D}));\delta^{L}_{X,A,C},\pi^{1}_{X,(A\times B)+(C\times D)};(\pi^{1}_{A,B}+\pi^{1}_{C,D})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle 1_{X}\times\mu_{A,B,C,D};\pi^{0}_{A+C,B+D});\delta^{L}_{X,A,C},\pi^{1}_{X,(A\times B)+(C\times D)};\mu_{A,B,C,D};\pi^{1}_{A+C,B+D}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle 1_{X}\times\mu_{A,B,C,D};\pi^{0}_{A+C,B+D});\delta^{L}_{X,A,C},(1_{X}\times\mu_{A,B,C,D});\pi^{1}_{X,(A+C)\times(B+D)};\pi^{1}_{A+C,B+D}\rangle

\displaystyle=(1_{X}\times\mu_{A,B,C,D});\langle(1_{X}\times\pi^{0}_{A+C,B+D});\delta^{L}_{X,A,C},\pi^{1}_{X,(A+C)\times(B+D)};\pi^{1}_{A+C,B+D}\rangle

\displaystyle=(1_{X}\times\mu_{A,B,C,D});\langle{\alpha_{\times}^{-1}}_{X,A+C,B+D};\pi^{0}_{X\times(A+C),B+D};\delta^{L}_{X,A,C},

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt{\alpha_{\times}^{-1}}_{X,A+C,B+D};\pi^{1}_{X\times(A+C),B+D}\rangle

\displaystyle=(1_{X}\times\mu_{A,B,C,D});{\alpha_{\times}^{-1}}_{X,A+C,B+D};\langle\pi^{0}_{X\times(A+C),B+D};\delta^{L}_{X,A,C},\pi^{1}_{X\times(A+C),B+D}\rangle

\displaystyle=(1_{X}\times\mu_{A,B,C,D});{\alpha_{\times}^{-1}}_{X,A+C,B+D};(\delta^{L}_{X,A,C}+1_{B+D})`

The second equality in (
21
) holds by the cocartesian par structure:

\displaystyle\mu_{A,B,C,D+X};(1_{A+C}\times{\alpha_{+}}_{B,D,X});\delta^{L}_{A+C,B+D,X}

\displaystyle=[(\iota^{0}_{A,C}\times\iota^{0}_{B,D+X});(1_{A+C}\times{\alpha_{+}}_{B,D,X});\delta^{L}_{A+C,B+D,X},(\iota^{1}_{A,C}\times\iota^{1}_{B,D+X});

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt(1_{A+C}\times{\alpha_{+}}_{B,D,X});\delta^{L}_{A+C,B+D,X})]

\displaystyle=[(\iota^{0}_{A,C}\times\iota^{0}_{B,D});(1_{A+C}\times\iota^{0}_{B+D,X});\delta^{L}_{A+C,B+D,X},(\iota^{1}_{A,C}\times(\iota^{1}_{B,D}+1_{X}));\delta^{L}_{A+C,B+D,X})]

\displaystyle=[(\iota^{0}_{A,C}\times\iota^{0}_{B,D});\iota^{0}_{(A+C)\times(B+D),X},\delta^{L}_{C,D,X};((\iota^{1}_{A,C}\times\iota^{1}_{B,D})+1_{X})]

\displaystyle=[\iota^{0}_{A\times B,C\times D};\mu_{A,B.C,D};\iota^{0}_{(A+C)\times(B+D),X},\delta^{L}_{C,D,X};(\iota^{1}_{A\times B,C\times D};\mu_{A,B,C,D}+1_{X})]

\displaystyle=[\iota^{0}_{A\times B,C\times D};\iota^{0}_{(A\times B)+(C\times D),X},\delta^{L}_{C,D,X};(\iota^{1}_{A\times B,C\times D}+1_{X})];(\mu_{A,B,C,D}+1_{X})

\displaystyle=[\iota^{0}_{A\times C,(C\times D)+X},\delta^{L}_{C,D,X};\iota^{1}_{A\times B,(C\times D)+X}];{\alpha_{+}}_{A\times B,C\times D,X};(\mu_{A,B,C,D}+1_{X})

\displaystyle=(1_{A\times B}+\delta^{L}_{C,D,X});{\alpha_{+}}_{A\times B,C\times D,X};(\mu_{A,B,C,D}+1_{X})

The last two equalities (
21
) hold via similar calculations using the right linear distributor instead.

\Box

Remark 3.13

Given that duoidal structure arises canonically when finite (co)products exist, any LDC with a (co)cartesian (resp. par) tensor structure is a duoidal category.

Recall that duoidal categories were first introduced in
[
16
]
to generalize the canonical interchange isomorphism in a braided monoidal category. While the duoidal interchange map is not in general an isomorphism, in a CLDC, we can use the mix maps to see that it is essentially the canonical interchange isomorphism for the (co)product:

\displaystyle\tau^{\times}_{W,X,Y,Z}=\langle\pi^{0}_{W,X}\times\pi^{0}_{Y,Z},\pi^{1}_{W,X}\times\pi^{1}_{Y,Z}\rangle\,\colon(W\times X)\times(Y\times Z)\rightarrow(W\times Y)\times(X\times Z)

\displaystyle\tau^{+}_{W,X,Y,Z}=[\iota^{0}_{W,Y}+\iota^{0}_{X,Z},\iota^{1}_{W,Y}+\iota^{1}_{X,Z}]\,\colon(W+X)+(Y+Z)\rightarrow(W+Y)+(X+Z)

Proposition 3.14

In a CLDC, the following diagrams commute:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 6.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 62.90808pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 104.81616pt\raise-71.08327pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.16458pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{mix}_{A,C}\times\mathrm{mix}_{B\times D}}$}}}\kern 6.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 104.81616pt\raise-77.7777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.8303pt\raise-93.4853pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.39374pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mu_{A,B,C,D}}$}}}\kern 6.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 62.90808pt\raise-85.16656pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 62.90808pt\raise-85.16656pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(A+C)\times(B+D)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(22)

Proof:

That the first diagram commutes follows from the naturality of the mix maps and the definitions of the interchanges:

\displaystyle\mu_{A,B,C,D};\mathrm{mix}_{A+C,B+D}

\displaystyle=[(\iota^{0}_{A,C}\times\iota^{0}_{B,D});\mathrm{mix}_{A+C,B+D},(\iota^{1}_{A,C}\times\iota^{1}_{B,D});\mathrm{mix}_{A+C,B+D}]

\displaystyle=[\mathrm{mix}_{A,B};(\iota^{0}_{A,C}+\iota^{0}_{B,D}),\mathrm{mix}_{C,D};(\iota^{0}_{A,C}+\iota^{0}_{B,D})]

\displaystyle=[\mathrm{mix}_{A,B};\iota^{0}_{A+B,C+D};\tau^{+}_{A,B,C,D},\mathrm{mix}_{C,D};\iota^{1}_{A+B,C+D};\tau^{+}_{A,B,C,D}]

\displaystyle=[\mathrm{mix}_{A,B};\iota^{0}_{A+B,C+D},\mathrm{mix}_{C,D};\iota^{1}_{A+B,C+D}];\tau^{+}_{A,B,C,D}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{mix}_{A,B}+\mathrm{mix}_{C,D});\tau^{+}_{A,B,C,D}

Similarly, we can also show that the second diagram commutes.

\Box

We conclude this section with the observation that in any category with finite products and finite coproducts, the interchange map is compatible with the symmetries in the sense that the following diagrams commute:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 52.60066pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-41.90808pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(A\times B)+(C\times D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.3303pt\raise 8.31874pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.39374pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mu_{A,B,C,D}}$}}}\kern 6.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.90808pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-52.60066pt\raise-14.28123pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.04999pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\sigma_{\times}}_{A,B}+{\sigma_{\times}}_{C,D}}$}}}\kern 6.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-20.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 65.90808pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.90808pt\raise-28.4375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern
