0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(A\times B)+(C\times D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.3303pt\raise-48.55626pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.39374pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mu_{A,B,C,D}}$}}}\kern 6.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.90808pt\raise-56.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-43.66704pt\raise-71.15623pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.39374pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\sigma_{+}}_{A\times B,C\times D}}$}}}\kern 6.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-77.87498pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 65.90808pt\raise-56.875pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(A+C)\times(B+D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 107.81616pt\raise-71.15623pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.04999pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{\sigma_{+}}_{A,C}\times{\sigma_{+}}_{B,D}}$}}}\kern 6.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 107.81616pt\raise-77.87498pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-41.90808pt\raise-85.3125pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(C\times D)+(A\times B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.3303pt\raise-93.63124pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 6.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.39374pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mu_{C,D,A,B}}$}}}\kern 6.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.90808pt\raise-85.3125pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 65.90808pt\raise-85.3125pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(C+A)\times(D+B)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(23)

which are easily shown using the (co)universal property of the (co)product and the (co)pairing definition of the interchange. As such, with these compatibilities and Prop
3.12
, it follows that every CLDC is also a symmetric
medial
linearly distributive category (MLDC). For more on MLDCs and how CLDCs can be characterize as special MLDCs, we invite the curious reader to see
[
17
]
.

4
Posetal Distributive Categories

In this section we discuss our first central class of CLDCs: posetal distributive categories. We also review how if a distributive category is a CLDC, then it must be posetal. Afterwards, we give an adjunction between CLDCs and posetal distributive categories using
semizero
objects.

4.1
Posetal and Distributive Categories

Let us first recall the definition of distributive categories.

Definition 4.1

[
5
, Sec 3]

A
distributive category
is a category
{\mathbb{D}}
with finite products and with finite coproducts such that the product distributes over the coproduct in the sense that the canonical natural transformations

\begin{gathered}d^{L}_{A,B,C}=[1_{A}\times\iota^{0}_{B,C},1_{A}\times\iota^{1}_{B,C}]\,\colon(A\times B)+(A\times C)\rightarrow A\times(B+C)\\
d^{R}_{A,B,C}=[\iota^{0}_{A,B}\times 1_{C},\iota^{1}_{A,B}\times 1_{C}]\,\colon(A\times C)+(B\times C)\rightarrow(A+B)\times C\end{gathered}

(24)

are isomorphisms.

Note of course that if one of the above natural transformations is an isomorphism, then so is the other one via symmetry.

Now, recall that a
posetal
category, otherwise known as a thin category, is a category such that for every
A
and
B
there is at most one map of type
A\to B
. If a map
A\to B
exists, then we say that
A\leq B
. We abuse notation and use
A\leq B
to also represent the map of type
A\to B
, and we write
A=B
for
A\cong B
, that is, when
A\leq B
and
B\leq A
.

Therefore, a posetal distributive category is a posetal category with terminal object
\top
, binary products
A\wedge B
(denoted by a meet in the posetal context), an initial object
\bot
, and binary coproducts
A\vee B
(denoted by a join in the posetal context), such that the product and coproduct distributive over one another in the sense that the following equality holds for all objects
A
,
B
, and
C
:

\displaystyle A\wedge(B\vee C)=(A\wedge B)\vee(A\wedge C)

\displaystyle A\vee(B\wedge C)=(A\vee B)\wedge(A\vee C)

(25)

Note that a small posetal distributive category is a
bounded distributive lattice
in the usual sense.

4.2
Posetal Collapse

Every posetal distributive category is a CLDC:

Example 4.2

A posetal distributive category
{\mathcal{L}}
is a CLDC, whose linear distributors are given by:

\displaystyle\delta^{R}_{A,B,C}\,\colon(A\vee B)\wedge C=(A\wedge C)\vee(B\wedge C)\leq A\vee(B\wedge C)

\displaystyle\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}\,\colon A\wedge(B\vee C)=(A\wedge B)\vee(A\wedge C)\leq(A\wedge B)\vee C

The linear distributor coherence conditions hold trivially as there is at most one map between two objects. In fact, posetal distributive category correspond precisely to posetal CLDCs.

Lemma 4.3

A CLDC is posetal if and only if it is a posetal distributive category.

Proof:
We have already seen the
\Leftarrow
direction in Prop
4.2
. For the
\Rightarrow
direction, suppose that we have a CLDC which is also posetal. In particular, the linear distributors tell us that:

(A\vee B)\wedge C\leq A\vee(B\wedge C)\hskip 18.49988ptA\wedge(B\vee C)\leq(A\wedge B)\vee C

The only axiom of a bounded distributive lattice category which is not immediate is distributivity (
25
). To show this, first note that
(A\wedge B)\vee(A\wedge C)\leq A\wedge(B\vee C)
is true whenever we have products and coproducts. In fact, this is the canonical natural transformation from the definition of a distributive category. On the other hand, using the linear distributors we get that:

\displaystyle A\wedge(B\vee C)

\displaystyle=(A\wedge A)\wedge(B\vee C)=A\wedge(A\wedge(B\vee C))\leq A\wedge((A\wedge B)\vee C)

\displaystyle=((A\wedge B)\vee C)\wedge A\leq(A\wedge B)\vee(C\wedge A)=(A\wedge B)\vee(A\wedge C)

Therefore it follows that
A\wedge(B\vee C)=(A\wedge B)\vee(A\wedge C)
. For the other distributive law, again first note that
A\vee(B\wedge C)\leq(A\vee B)\wedge(A\vee C)
holds whenever we have products and coproducts. Using the linear distributor we also have that:

\displaystyle\begin{split}(A\vee B)\wedge(A\vee C)&=(A\vee B)\wedge(C\vee A)\leq((A\vee B)\wedge C)\vee A\leq(A\vee(B\wedge C))\vee A\\
&=A\vee(A\vee(B\wedge C))=(A\vee A)\vee(B\wedge C)=A\vee(B\wedge C)\end{split}

So
A\vee(B\wedge C)=(A\vee B)\wedge(A\vee C)
. So we conclude that a posetal CLDC is a posetal distributive category.

\Box

There are two well-known theorems describing how certain kinds of CLDCs collapse to being posetal: Joyal’s paradox and “orthogonality of linear distributivity and standard distributivity”. We will take the time to detail these results here, as they are integral to our understanding of CLDCs.

We begin with Joyal’s Paradox, one of the most famous results in categorical logic. Joyal’s Paradox states that a small cartesian closed category with involution is a Boolean algebra, in other words is posetal. This means that the naive definition for categorical semantics of classical logic, generalizing the categorical semantics of intuitionistic logic to the classical case, just provide semantics of provability and not proofs. For an in-depth discussion of Joyal’s Paradox in its standard form, see
[
13
, Appendix B]
. We shall state the result here using our language of CLDCs.

As before, let us consider a posetal distributive category, but this time add complements: for every object
A
, there is an object
A^{\ast}
such that
A\vee A^{\ast}=\top
and
A\wedge A^{\ast}=\bot
. Note that a small complemented posetal distributive category is precisely a complemented bounded distributive lattice, otherwise known as a
Boolean lattice
.

Theorem 4.4
(Joyal’s Paradox)

A CLDC has negation if and only if it is a complemented posetal distributive category.

Proof:

For the
\Leftarrow
direction, a complemented posetal distributive category is by definition a posetal distributive category, and hence a posetal CLDC by Lemma
4.3
. It is immediate that it has negation by setting
A^{\perp}=A^{\ast}
.

For
\Rightarrow
direction, consider a CLDC
{\mathbb{X}}
with negation. For every object
A
we have a complementation pair
(A,A^{\perp},\gamma^{R}_{A},\tau^{L}_{A})
. Now for every other object
B
we have that
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}(A,B)\cong\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}(A\times\top,B)\cong\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}(\top,A^{\perp}+B)
. By Prop
3.5
,
\top
is preinitial, meaning that there is at most one map of type
\top\to A^{\perp}+B
. As such, there is at most one map of type
A\to B
, or in other words,
A
is preinitial. As this is true for all objects
A
in
{\mathbb{X}}
, there is at most one morphism between two objects, thus
{\mathbb{X}}
is a posetal. Hence by Lemma
4.3
,
{\mathbb{X}}
is a posetal distributive category. Setting
A^{\ast}=A^{\perp}
, since we have a complementation pair, we have that
\top\leq A\vee A^{\ast}
and
A\wedge A^{\ast}\leq\bot
. Now,
A\vee A^{\ast}\leq\top
and
\bot\leq A\wedge A^{\ast}
as
\top
is terminal and
\bot
is initial. Thus,
A\vee A^{\ast}=\top
and
A\wedge A^{\ast}=\bot
. Therefore,
{\mathbb{X}}
is also a complemented posetal distributive category, as desired.

\Box

Of course, if we restrict our attention to small CLDCs, we get that: a small CLDC has negation if and only if it is a Boolean algebra.

We now turn our attention to the other collapse, which involves distributive categories. As previously mentioned, it was initially thought that all distributive categories were CLDCs. However, it later became clear that this is only true if the distributive category is posetal. This now very well-known result was given by Cockett and Seely in the corrected version of “Weakly Distributive Categories”
[
8
]
. We give an alternative proof of this result using a characterization of preinitial objects in a distributive category and our result that the terminal object in a CLDC is preinitial. This gives a nice simple alternative proof of the collapse in question.

Proposition 4.5

[
5
, Prop 3.3]

For an object
A
in a distributive category, the following are equivalent:

(i)

A
is preinitial,

(ii)

There is an object
B
such that
\iota^{0}_{A,B}\,\colon A\rightarrow A+B
is an isomorphism,

(iii)

t_{A};\iota^{0}_{\top,\top}=t_{A};\iota^{1}_{\top,\top}\,\colon A\rightarrow\top+\top

Proposition 4.6

[
8
, Prop 3.1]

A CLDC is a distributive category if and only if it is posetal.

Proof:
The
\Leftarrow
direction is immediate from Lemma
4.3
: a posetal CLDC is a posetal distributive category, which is of course a distributive category. For the
\Rightarrow
direction, suppose that a CLDC is also a distributive category. Then for all objects
A\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
, by Prop
3.5
and Lemma
3.4
, we get that
t_{A};\iota^{0}_{\top,\top}=t_{A};\iota^{1}_{\top,\top}
as
\iota^{0}_{\top,\top}=\iota^{1}_{\top,\top}
Therefore, by Prop
4.5
, every object
A
is preinitial, implying
{\mathbb{X}}
is posetal.

\Box

In fact, we do not require the inverses of all canonical maps
d^{L}_{A,B,C}
to prove the CLDC is posetal. This is due to the fact that the proof of
(iii)\Rightarrow(i)
of Prop
4.5
only requires distributivity over
\top+\top
, as remarked by Cockett in
[
5
]
. In other words:

Lemma 4.7

Let
A
be an object in a cartesian and cocartesian category. If the map

d^{L}_{A,\top,\top}=[1_{A}\times\iota^{0}_{\top,\top},1_{A}\times\iota^{1}_{\top,\top}]

has a right inverse and
t_{A};\iota^{0}_{\top,\top}=t_{A};\iota^{1}_{\top,\top}
, then
A
is preinitial.

Proof:

Consider the following commuting diagram:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern This implies
\Delta_{A};\pi^{0}_{A,A};\iota^{0}_{A,A}=\Delta_{A};\pi^{0}_{A,A};\iota^{1}_{A,A}
and thus
\iota^{0}_{A,A}=\iota^{1}_{A,A}
.
A
is then preinitial by Lemma
3.4
.

\Box

Then, using the above lemma instead of Prop
4.5
, we get:

Lemma 4.8

A CLDC is posetal if and only if the following canonical natural transformation:

d^{L}_{A,\top,\top}=[1_{A}\times\iota^{0}_{\top,\top},1_{A}\times\iota^{1}_{\top,\top}]\,\colon(A\times\top)+(A\times\top)\rightarrow A\times(\top+\top)

has a right inverse.

An important remark is that in any CLDC, there is a potential reasonable candidate
{d^{L}}^{\flat}:A\times(B+C)\to(A\times B)+(A\times C)
which could be the inverse to the canonical natural transformation
d^{L}_{A,B,C}:(A\times B)+(A\times C)\to A\times(B+C)
, which is defined to be the following composite:

(26)

By Proposition
4.6
, we see that this candidate is, in fact, the inverse only when the CLDC is posetal. Cockett and Seely further prove an additional characterization of when said candidate is the inverse: when the initial object is
strict
, which means that all maps to it are isomorphisms (and dually a terminal object is
costrict
if all maps from it are isomorphisms).

, Lem 3.2]

then the canonical natural transformation

Remark 4.10

[
8
, Lemma 3.2]
includes an extra diagram in its assumptions, but this diagram is simply the second equation of (
15
), which holds in any CLDC. Moreover, the lemma states that a CLDC is distributive if and only if the diagrams commute, but only prove that the candidate morphism (
26
) is a right inverse. Nevertheless, the right inverse is all that is needed to prove the collapse.

We can now state:

Proposition 4.11

[
8
, Thm 3.3]

A CLDC is posetal if and only if the initial object is strict.

Proof:

For the
\Rightarrow
direction, a CLDC being posetal implies that the initial object is strict. For the
\Leftarrow
direction, we can show that if we have a strict initial object, then (
27
) holds, as detailed in the proof of
[
8
, Thm 3.3]
. This means that
d^{L}_{A,B,C}
has a right inverse by the above lemmma, and so in particular
d^{L}_{A,\top,\top}
has a right inverse. Thus by Lemma
4.8
, we conclude that the CLDC is posetal.

\Box

If we consider the opposite CLDC (the opposite category with the roles of tensor and par exchanged), we get a dual statement about costrict terminal objects. Compiling all these characterizations together gives the following collapse theorem:

Theorem 4.12

For a CLDC
{\mathbb{X}}
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

{\mathbb{X}}
is a posetal distributive category;

(ii)

{\mathbb{X}}
is a posetal category;

(iii)

{\mathbb{X}}
is a distributive category;

(iv)

{\mathbb{X}}
has a strict initial object;

(v)

{\mathbb{X}}
has a costrict terminal object.

4.3
Semizero Objects

We conclude this section by showing that from any CLDC we can construct a posetal distributive category. We do so by considering the subcategory of
semizero
objects. In an arbitrary category, a semizero object is an object that is both preinitial and subterminal (in the same way that a zero object is n object that is both terminal and initial). Of course, recall that in a CLDC, by Prop
3.10
, being subterminal is equivalent to being preinitial. So we can immediately extend Prop
3.10
to include semizero objects.

Lemma 4.13

In a CLDC, an object
A
is a semizero object if and only if
A
is preinitial (or equivalently subterminal).

Now for a category
{\mathbb{X}}
, we let
\mbox{${\mathrm{SZ}}$}[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
be the full subcategory of semizero objects of
{\mathbb{X}}
. By the above lemma, for a CLDC this subcategory is equivalent to taking the subcategory of preinitial objects (or equivalently subterminal objects).

Proposition 4.14

For a CLDC
{\mathbb{X}}
,
\mbox{${\mathrm{SZ}}$}[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
is a posetal CLDC, so in particular it is a posetal distributive category.

Proof:

Since there is at most one map between semizero objects, for any category
{\mathbb{X}}
, we have that
\mbox{${\mathrm{SZ}}$}[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
is always a posetal category. So suppose that
{\mathbb{X}}
is also a CLDC. To show that
\mbox{${\mathrm{SZ}}$}[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
is a CLDC, it suffices to show that the (co)product of semizero objects is again semizero, and that both the terminal object and initial object are semizero objects. However, recall from Prop
3.5
that
\top
is preinitial and
\bot
is subterminal, and therefore it follows that indeed
\top
and
\bot
are semizero objects. Next it follows from Lemma
3.4
, that in a (co)cartesian category, the (co)product of (resp. preinitial) subterminal objects is again (resp. preinitial) subterminal. Thus from this fact and Prop
3.10
, it follows that in a CLDC, if
A
and
B
are semizero objects, then
A+B
and
A\times B
are also semizero objects. Therefore, from here we can conclude that
\mbox{${\mathrm{SZ}}$}[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
is a sub-CLDC of
{\mathbb{X}}
, and hence by Lemma
4.3
,
\mbox{${\mathrm{SZ}}$}[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
is a posetal distributive category.

\Box

We now show that taking the subcategory of semizero objects of a CLDC provides the right adjoint to the inclusion functor of posetal distributive categories into CLDCs. To state this properly, we must first address morphisms between posetal distributive categories. These will correspond to the categorical version of lattice homomorphisms
[
15
, Sec I.1.]
. So for posetal distributive categories
{\mathcal{B}}
and
{\mathcal{L}}
, a
posetal distributive functor
is a functor
F\,\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{L}}
which strictly preserves products, coproducts, the terminal object, and the initial object:

\displaystyle F(A\wedge B)=F(A)\wedge F(B)

\displaystyle F(\top)=\top

\displaystyle F(A\vee B)=F(A)\vee F(B)

\displaystyle F(\bot)=\bot

It is straightforward to see that when seeing a posetal distributive category as a posetal CLDC, a posetal distributive functor is the same thing as Frobenius cartesian linear functor. So let
\mathbf{PDC}
denote the category of posetal distributive categories and posetal distributive functors, which can equivalently be described as the category of posetal CLDC and Frobenius cartesian linear functors.

Now to build our functor from
\mathbf{PDC}
to
\mathbf{FCLDC}
, we first show that, unsurprisingly, cartesian linear functor preserve semizero objects, which then implies that Frobenius cartesian linear functors restrict to the subcategory of semizero objects.

Lemma 4.15

Cartesian linear functors preserve semizero objects.

Proof:
It follows from Lemma
3.4
that if a functor
F
preserves (co)products, and
A
is a (resp. preinitial) subterminal object, then
F(A)
is also (resp. preinitial) subterminal. Since a cartesian linear functor
F=(F_{\times},F_{+})\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
consists of a functor
F_{\times}
and
F_{+}
which preserves products and coproduct respectively, if
A
is a semizero object in
{\mathbb{X}}
, then
F_{\times}(A)
and
F_{+}(A)
are both semizero objects in CLDC
{\mathbb{Y}}
.

\Box

Corollary 4.16

A Frobenius cartesian linear functor
F\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\to\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
restricts to a posetal distributive functor
F\,\colon\mbox{${\mathrm{SZ}}$}[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathrm{SZ}}$}[\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}]
.

As such, we can now define the functor
\mbox{${\mathrm{SZ}}$}[-]\,\colon\mbox{$\mathbf{FCLDC}$}\rightarrow\mathbf{PDC}
which maps a CLDC to its subcategory of semizero objects, and a Frobenius cartesian linear functor to its restriction on the subcategory of semizero objects. On the other hand, let
U\,\colon\mathbf{PDC}\rightarrow\mbox{$\mathbf{FCLDC}$}
be inclusion functor, or in other words, the forgetful functor.

Theorem 4.17

\mbox{${\mathrm{SZ}}$}[-]\,\colon\mbox{$\mathbf{FCLDC}$}\rightarrow\mathbf{PDC}
is a right adjoint of
U\,\colon\mathbf{PDC}\rightarrow\mbox{$\mathbf{FCLDC}$}
.

Proof:
Let
{\mathbb{X}}
be a CLDC and
{\mathcal{L}}
a posetal distributive category. Now note that every object in
{\mathcal{L}}
is semizero, as such
\mbox{${\mathrm{SZ}}$}[{\mathcal{L}}]={\mathcal{L}}
. Then given a Frobenius cartesian linear functor
F\,\colon{\mathcal{L}}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
, this clearly gives a posetal distributive functor
F\,\colon\mbox{${\mathrm{SZ}}$}[{\mathcal{L}}]\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathrm{SZ}}$}[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
. On the other hand, given a posetal distributive functor
G\,\colon{\mathcal{L}}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathrm{SZ}}$}[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]
, this extends to a Frobenius cartesian linear functor
{\mathcal{L}}\to\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
by post-composing
G
with the inclusion functor
\mbox{${\mathrm{SZ}}$}[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}]\hookrightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
. It is clear that this gives a natural bijection
\mathbf{PDC}({\mathcal{L}},\mbox{${\mathrm{SZ}}$}[\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}])\cong\mbox{$\mathbf{FCLDC}$}(U({\mathcal{L}}),\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$})
.

\Box

5
Semi-Additive Categories

In this section, we discuss our second main class of CLDC examples:
semi-additive
categories, which turn out to be the compact CLDCs. Recall that a semi-additive category is essentially a category with finite
biproducts
. The main result of this section is a collapse result which says that if a CLDC is isomix if and only if it is a semi-additive category. We also provide a construction of a semi-additive category from a CLDC via the (co)slice category over the initial (resp. terminal) object.

5.1
Semi-Additive Categories and Biproducts

If only to the setup notation and terminology, it may be useful to quickly review semi-additive categories. First recall that in an arbitrary category, a
zero object
is an object
{\bf 0}
, which is both a terminal and initial object. A useful observation is that having a zero object is equivalent to having a terminal object and an initial object that are isomorphic.

Lemma 5.1

A category has a zero object if and only if it has an initial object
\bot
and a terminal object
\top
, and the unique map
b_{\top}=t_{\bot}\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
is an isomorphism.

If a category has a zero object, then it has
zero morphisms
, which recall is the unique map between every pair of objects
X
and
Y
which factors through the zero object:
0_{X,Y}\,\colon X\xrightarrow{t_{X}}{\bf 0}\xrightarrow{b_{Y}}Y
. These zero morphisms are absorbing in the sense that for all maps
f\,\colon X\to Y
,
f;0_{Y,Y^{\prime}}=0_{X,Y^{\prime}}
and
0_{X^{\prime},X};f=0_{X^{\prime},Y}
. Now if a category with a zero object also has finite products and finite coproducts, then there is a canonical natural transformation
\psi_{X,Y}\,\colon X+Y\to X\times Y
defined as follows:

\displaystyle\psi_{X,Y}=[\langle 1_{X},0_{X,Y}\rangle,\langle 0_{Y,X},1_{Y}\rangle]=\langle[1_{X},0_{Y,X}],[0_{X,Y},1_{Y}]\rangle\,\colon X+Y\rightarrow X\times Y

(28)

Definition 5.2

[
18
]

A
semi-additive
category is a category with a zero object
{\bf 0}
, with finite products and finite coproducts, such that
\psi_{X,Y}\,\colon X+Y\to X\times Y
is a natural isomorphism.

As such, we will call
\psi
the
semi-additive comparison
. Then a
semi-additive category
is a category where its semi-additive comparison is an isomorphism. It is worth mentioning that any isomorphisms between binary coproducts and binary products is sufficient for a category to be semi-additive.

Proposition 5.3

[
18
, Thm 5]

If a category has finite products and finite coproducts and a natural isomorphism
A+B\to A\times B
, then it is semi-additive.

Now in a semi-additive category, since
A+B\cong A\times B
, this implies that
+
is also a product and
\times
is also a coproduct, and, in fact, both are a biproduct. Of course, one can define biproducts directly. Recall that in a category with a zero object, a
biproduct
of
A
and
B
is an object equipped with four maps:

\pi^{0}_{A,B}\,\colon A\oplus B\rightarrow A\hskip 18.49988pt\pi^{1}_{A,B}\,\colon A\oplus B\rightarrow B\hskip 18.49988pt\iota^{0}_{A,B}\,\colon A\rightarrow A\oplus B\hskip 18.49988pt\iota^{1}_{A,B}\,\colon B\rightarrow A\oplus B

such that
A\oplus B
equipped with
\pi^{0}_{A,B}
and
\pi^{1}_{A,B}
is a product,
A\oplus B
equipped with
\iota^{0}_{A,B}
and
\iota^{1}_{A,B}
is a coproduct, and the following diagrams commute:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 283.2106pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 114.7253pt\raise-30.8375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 158.76157pt\raise-30.8375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 195.35036pt\raise-30.8375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 239.64235pt\raise-30.8375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 276.74258pt\raise-30.8375pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

(29)

Then a bicartesian category is a category with finite biproducts. Every bicartesian category
{\mathbb{X}}
is a symmetric monoidal category
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\oplus,{\bf 0})
. Equivalently, a symmetric monoidal category is bicartesian if its monoidal structure is both cartesian and cocartesian. Of course, every bicartesian category is a semi-additive category where
\top={\bf 0}=\bot
and
\times=\oplus=+
, and
\psi_{A,B}=1_{A\oplus B}
. Conversely, every semi-additive category is a bicartesian category by setting
{\bf 0}=\top
or
{\bf 0}=\bot
, and
\oplus=\times
or
\oplus=+
.

We can also ask what should morphisms of semi-additive categories be. These should be functors that preserves both products and coproducts, but are also compatible with the semi-additive comparison map.

Definition 5.4

A functor
F\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
between semi-additive categories is
semi-additive
if

(i)

(F,m_{\bf 0},m_{\times})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\times,{\bf 0})\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},\times,{\bf 0})
is a monoidal functor;

(ii)

(F,n_{\bf 0},n_{+})\,\colon(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},+,{\bf 0})\rightarrow(\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$},+,{\bf 0})
is a monoidal functor

and such that the following diagrams commute.

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.36113pt\hbox{\kern 31.8964pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-23.03717pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F(A+B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern (30)

We denote the category of semi-additive categories and semi-additive functors by
\mathbf{SAdd}
.

5.2
Semi-Additive Collapse

Every semi-additive category is a CLDC:

Example 5.5

A semi-additive category, with natural isomorphism
\psi_{A,B}\,\colon A+B\cong A\times B
, is a CLDC whose linear distributors are defined as follows:

6.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 215.5794pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 215.5794pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.36113pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(A\times B)+C}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

It is straightforward (though very tedious) to check that the linear distributor axioms hold. These essentially amount to the cartesian monoidal structure and cocartesian monoidal structure being associative, unital, and symmetric, and also isomorphic to each other. Moreover, a semi-additive category is a compact CLDC, where the unital mix map is the zero morphism
m\,\colon=0_{\bot,\top}\,\colon\bot\to\top
, and the induced mix map
m_{A,B}\,\colon A\times B\to A+B
is
m_{A,B}=\psi^{-1}_{A,B}
.

This implies that every bicartesian category is a degenerate CLDC.

Example 5.6

A bicartesian category is a degenerate CLDC, so in particular its linear distributors are given by the associators of the biproduct,
A\oplus(B\oplus C)\cong(A\oplus B)\oplus C
.

In fact, semi-additive (resp. bicartesian) categories correspond precisely to the compact (resp. degenerate) CLDCs.

Lemma 5.7

A CLDC is compact if and only if it is a semi-additive category.

Proof:
The
\Leftarrow
direction is Ex
5.5
. For the
\Rightarrow
direction, suppose that
{\mathbb{X}}
is a compact CLDC, so
\bot\cong\top
and
A\times B\cong A+B
. However by Prop
5.3
, since the mix maps give a natural isomorphism from the coproduct to the product, this implies that
{\mathbb{X}}
is a semi-additive category. It is easy to check that the linear distributors must be of the form as given in Ex
5.5
and that
\psi_{A,B}=\mathrm{mix}^{-1}_{A,B}
.

\Box

Corollary 5.8

A CLDC is degenerate if and only if it is a bicartesian category.

Moreover, it is straightforward to see that from the point of view of CLDCs, semi-additive functors correspond precisely to Frobenius cartesian linear functors.

Lemma 5.9

The notion of semi-additive functors is equivalent to Frobenius cartesian linear functors between semi-additive categories (seen as a compact CLDC).

Proof:
It is clear that a Frobenius cartesian linear functor is a semi-additive functor. Conversely, consider a semi-additive functor
F\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{B}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{B}}$}^{\prime}
between semi-additive categories. To prove that
F
is a Frobenius cartesian linear functor, it remains only to show that (
12
) holds. The first condition holds by commutativity of the following diagram:

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.31248pt\hbox{\kern 58.53731pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-39.504pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F(A)\times F(B+C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.44528pt\raise 7.11867pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 4.79993pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.39374pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{m_{\times}}_{A,B+C}}$}}}\kern 4.79993pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 97.67917pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-48.18628pt\raise-20.28123pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 4.79993pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.865pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{1_{F(A)}\times{n_{+}}_{B,C}}$}}}\kern 4.79993pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-32.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise The second condition follows similarly. Thus
F\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{B}}$}\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{B}}$}^{\prime}
is a Frobenius cartesian linear functor.

\Box

As such, this gives us a forgetful functor
U\,\colon\mathbf{SAdd}\rightarrow\mbox{$\mathbf{FCLDC}$}
.

The objective for the remainder of this section is to show that a CLDC is isomix if and only if it is semi-additive. Equivalently, this amounts to saying that a CLDC has a zero object if and only if it its products are isomorphic to its coproducts. In particular, from a LDC point of view, this says that in a CLDC, if its tensor unit and par unit are isomorphic, then tensor and par are also isomorphic. This is quite a strong collapse since recall that an LDC can be isomix
\top\cong\bot
without the mix map
A\invamp B\to A\otimes B
being an isomorphism.

To show this, we first show that if the linear distributors are isomorphisms, then a CLDC is a semi-additive category (and hence compact).

Proposition 5.10

A CLDC has invertible linear distributors if and only if it is a semi-additive category.

Proof:
The
\Leftarrow
direction is immediate since the linear distributors as defined in Ex
5.5
are the composites of isomorphisms. For the
\Rightarrow
direction, let
{\mathbb{X}}
be a CLDC whose linear distributors are isomorphisms. By Proposition
2.5
, this implies that
\bot
has
\times
-inverse. However, note that in a cartesian category, an object has a
\times
-inverse if and only if it is terminal, that is, isomorphic to
\top
. Indeed, suppose
A
had a
\times
-inverse
A^{-1}
, which means that
t_{A\times A^{-1}}:A\times A^{-1}\to\top
is an isomorphism. Then consider the composite
t^{-1}_{A\times A^{-1}};\pi^{0}_{A,A^{-1}}:\top\to A
. Then by the universal property of the terminal object, we of course have that
t^{-1}_{A\times A^{-1}};\pi^{0}_{A,A^{-1}};t_{A}=t_{\top}=1_{\top}
. On the other hand, note that by the universal property of the terminal object, we get that
t_{A}=\langle 1_{A},t^{-1}_{A\times A^{-1}};\pi^{1}_{A,A^{-1}}\rangle;t_{A\times A^{-1}}
. So we can compute that:

\displaystyle t_{A};t^{-1}_{A\times A^{-1}};\pi^{0}_{A,A^{-1}}=\langle 1_{A},t^{-1}_{A\times A^{-1}};\pi^{1}_{A,A^{-1}}\rangle;t_{A\times A^{-1}}t^{-1}_{A\times A^{-1}};\pi^{0}_{A,A^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\langle 1_{A},t^{-1}_{A\times A^{-1}};\pi^{1}_{A,A^{-1}}\rangle\pi^{0}_{A,A^{-1}}=1_{A}

So we conclude that
t_{A}:A\to\top
is an isomorphism. Therefore, since
\bot
has a
\times
-inverse, it is terminal and so
\bot\cong\top
. The mix maps
\mathrm{mix}_{A,B}\,\colon A\times B\rightarrow A+B
are isomorphisms in this context as they are defined as composites of unitors, linear distributors, and the nullary mix map
m
, which are all isomorphisms. Therefore, the mix maps are isomorphisms and
{\mathbb{X}}
is a compact CLDC, and thus by Lemma
5.7
,
{\mathbb{X}}
is a semi-additive category.

\Box

We can now show that a CLDC is isomix implies that it is compact.

Proposition 5.11

A CLDC is isomix if and only if it is a semi-additive category.

Proof:
The
\Leftarrow
direction is immediate as a semi-additive category has a zero object, For the
\Rightarrow
direction, suppose that
{\mathbb{X}}
is an isomix CLDC, so the nullary mix map
m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
is invertible. We shall show that the left linear distributor
\delta^{L}
is invertible. Define the natural transformation
\partial^{R}_{A,B,C}\,\colon(A\times B)+C\rightarrow A\times(B+C)
as follows:

\displaystyle\partial^{R}_{A,B,C}=\langle[\pi^{0}_{A,B},t_{C};m^{-1};b_{A}],\pi^{1}_{A,B}+1_{C}\rangle=[1_{A}\times\iota^{0}_{B,C},\langle t_{C};m^{-1};b_{A},\iota^{1}_{B,C}\rangle]

First observe that the following diagram commutes:

185.32893pt\raise-92.161pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 4.79993pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.14684pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{u^{R}_{\times}}^{-1}_{A}}$}}}\kern 4.79993pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 220.00195pt\raise-110.63766pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 223.46997pt\raise-85.0625pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-14.69019pt\raise-113.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\times\top\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 106.09875pt\raise-121.37177pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 4.79993pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.14684pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{{u^{R}_{\times}}^{-1}_{A}}$}}}\kern 4.79993pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 220.00195pt\raise-113.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern-0.18243pt\raise 0.98322pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern 0.18243pt\raise-0.98322pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern-0.18243pt\raise 0.98322pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern 0.18243pt\raise-0.98322pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern-0.18243pt\raise 0.98322pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern 0.18243pt\raise-0.98322pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}{\hbox{\kern 73.22543pt\raise-113.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 147.24454pt\raise-113.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 220.00195pt\raise-113.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.31248pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

where
(*)
commutes by

\displaystyle((1_{A}\times t_{B})+t_{C});(1_{A\times\top}+m^{-1});{u^{R}_{+}}_{A\times\top};{u^{R}_{\times}}^{-1}_{A}

\displaystyle=[(1_{A}\times t_{B});\iota^{0}_{A\times\top,\bot},t_{C};m^{-1};\iota^{1}_{A\times\top,\bot}];[1_{A\times\top},b_{A\times\top}];{u^{R}_{\times}}^{-1}_{A}

\displaystyle=[(1_{A}\times t_{B});{u^{R}_{\times}}^{-1}_{A},t_{C};m^{-1};b_{A\times\top};{u^{R}_{\times}}^{-1}_{A}]=[\pi^{0}_{A,B},t_{C};m^{-1};b_{A}]

As such, we can compute that:

\displaystyle\delta^{L}_{A,B,C};\partial^{R}_{A,B,C}=\delta^{L}_{A,B,C};\langle[\pi^{0}_{A,B},t_{C};m^{-1};b_{A}],\pi^{1}_{A,B}+1_{C}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\delta^{L}_{A,B,C};[\pi^{0}_{A,B},t_{C};m^{-1};b_{A}],\delta^{L}_{A,B,C};(\pi^{1}_{A,B}+1_{C})\rangle=\langle\pi^{0}_{A,B+C},\pi^{1}_{A,B+C}\rangle=1_{A\times(B+C)}

where the equality in the first component follows from the above diagram, and the equality in the second component is by Lemma
3.2
. Similarly, we can also compute that:

\displaystyle\partial^{R}_{A,B,C};\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}=[1_{A}\times\iota^{0}_{B,C},\langle t_{C};m^{-1};b_{A},\iota^{1}_{B,C}\rangle];\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}

\displaystyle=[(1_{A}\times\iota^{0}_{B,C});\delta^{L}_{A,B,C},\langle t_{C};m^{-1};b_{A},\iota^{1}_{B,C}\rangle;\delta^{L}_{A,B,C}]=[\iota^{0}_{A\times B,C},\iota^{1}_{A\times B,C}]=1_{A\times(B+C)}

Thus
\delta^{L}
is an isomorphism with inverse
\partial^{R}
. Consequently, via the symmetries, the right linear distributor
\delta^{R}
is invertible as well. Then by Prop
5.10
,
{\mathbb{X}}
is a semi-additive category. The inverse of the mix map
m_{A,B}:A\times B\to A+B
is:

\mathrm{mix}_{A,B}^{-1}=\langle[1_{A},t_{B};m^{-1};b_{A}],[t_{A};m^{-1};b_{B},1_{B}]\rangle=[\langle 1_{A},t_{A};m^{-1};b_{B}\rangle,\langle t_{B};m^{-1};b_{A},1_{B}\rangle]

which is precisely
\psi_{A,B}
.

\Box

Combining these characterizations together, we record that:

Theorem 5.12

For a CLDC
{\mathbb{X}}
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

{\mathbb{X}}
is a semi-additive category;

(ii)

{\mathbb{X}}
is compact;

(iii)

{\mathbb{X}}
has invertible linear distributors;

(iv)

{\mathbb{X}}
is isomix.

It is worth mentioning that some of the constructions used in the above proofs connect back to duoidal categories. Indeed, a
normal
duiodal category, that is, a duoidal
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},\diamond,I,\star,J)
such that
\iota\,\colon I\rightarrow J
is an isomorphism, is an isomix LDC
[
21
]
, whose linear distributors are defined as follows:

\partial^{L}_{A,B,C}=A\diamond(B\star C)\cong(A\star J)\diamond(B\star C)\xrightarrow{\mu_{A,J,B,C}}(A\diamond B)\star(J\diamond C)\cong(A\diamond B)\star(I\diamond C)\cong(A\diamond B)\star C

\partial^{R}_{A,B,C}=(A\star B)\diamond C\cong(A\star B)\diamond(J\star C)\xrightarrow{\mu_{A,B,J,C}}(A\diamond J)\star(B\diamond C)\cong(A\diamond I)\star(B\diamond C)\cong A\star(B\diamond C)

Now, for any isomix CLDC
{\mathbb{X}}
(so a semi-additive category),
(\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$},+,\bot,\times,\top)
is normal duoidal. The induced linear distributors from this normal duoidal category are precisely the inverses of
\delta^{R}
and
\delta^{L}
constructed in the proof of Thm
5.11
. As such this makes
{\mathbb{X}}
also an isomix ‘‘cocartesian’’ linearly distributive category
1
1

1

Note that the opposite category of a CLDC is not a cocartesian LDC, and vice-versa.
, where by cocartesian LDC we mean one where the coproduct is the tensor and product is par.

Finally, it is interesting to notice that our two main classes of CLDCs discussed so far are “orthogonal” to one another.

Proposition 5.13

A CLDC is a semi-additive category and a posetal distributive category if and only if it is trivial.

Proof:

The
\Leftarrow
direction is trivially true. On the other hand, the
\Rightarrow
direction follows from the fact that for a posetal distributive category its initial object
\bot
is strict, while for a semi-additive category its initial object
\bot
is a zero objet. Therefore, for any object
A
, the unique zero map
0_{A,\bot}:A\to\bot
must be an isomorphism. So every object is a zero object, and so the CLDC is trivial.

\Box

Viewing CLDCs as models for some logic with two connectives, two constants and the cut rule, the posetal distributive categories correspond to all proofs between two formulas being identified, while the semi-additive categories correspond to the connectives being identified.

5.3
(Co)Slice Construction

In this section, we show that for a CLDC, the (co)slice category over the initial (resp. terminal) object is an isomix CLDC, and so in particular a semi-additive category. In fact, this (co)slice category is equivalent to a certain subcategory. As such, there are at least two full subcategories of a CLDC which are semi-additive categories. However, these subcategories will not necessarily be sub-CLDCs of the original CLDC, since the subcategories may not have the same initial or terminal object as the CLDC.

While we will focus on the slice category over the initial object, the same dual arguments of course hold for the coslice category over the terminal object. So recall that for a category
{\mathbb{X}}
and object
X
, the
slice category

\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/X
is the category whose objects are maps
f\,\colon A\rightarrow X
with codomain
X
, where a map
g\,\colon(f\,\colon A\rightarrow X)\rightarrow(f^{\prime}\,\colon A^{\prime}\rightarrow X)
is a map
g\,\colon A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
in
{\mathbb{X}}
such that
g;f^{\prime}=f
.

Now suppose that
{\mathbb{X}}
is a CLDC and consider the slice category over its initial object
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
. Recall that by Prop
3.5
,
\bot
is subterminal. Therefore, for every object
A
, there is at most one map into
\bot
. So having such a map is a property of an object
A
rather than structure. Moreover, if
A
and
A^{\prime}
both have maps into
\bot
, then any map
g:A\to A^{\prime}
will also preserve said maps. Thus
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
is isomorphic to the full subcategory of objects for which there exists (a necessarily) unique map into
\bot
. Abusing notation, we will simply associate
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
to this subcategory. Therefore, from now on, for a CLDC
{\mathbb{X}}
,
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
will be the full subcategory of object
A
of
{\mathbb{X}}
for which there exists (a necessarily) unique map into
\bot
.

Proposition 5.14

Let
{\mathbb{X}}
be a CLDC. Then
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
is a compact CLDC, and therefore a semi-additive category.

Proof:
We will in fact explain why
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
is semi-additive. First we will explain why it has the same products, coproducts, and initial object as
{\mathbb{X}}
. We only need to show this is true for objects, since being a full subcategory will immediately imply their respective (co)universal property. Starting with the latter, obviously
\bot\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
. Now if
A,A^{\prime}\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
with respective maps
a\,\colon A\rightarrow\bot
and
a^{\prime}\,\colon A^{\prime}\rightarrow\bot
, then so are
A\times A^{\prime}
and
A+A^{\prime}
via maps
(a\times a^{\prime});\pi^{0}_{\bot,\bot}:A\times A^{\prime}\to\bot\times\bot\to\bot
and
[a,a^{\prime}]:A+A^{\prime}\to\bot
respectively.

Now note that it is easy to see that
\bot
is in fact a zero object in
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
(in fact this, along with the coproducts, follow from basic facts about slice categories). Moreover, if
A,A^{\prime}\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
, then the zero morphism from
A
to
A^{\prime}
in this category is the composite
a;b_{A^{\prime}}
. As such since
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
has a zero object, products, and coproducts, we get our canonical map
\psi_{A,A^{\prime}}\,\colon A+A^{\prime}\to A\times A^{\prime}
, which is worked out to be:

\displaystyle\psi_{A,A^{\prime}}=\langle[1_{A},b;b_{A}],[a;b_{A^{\prime}},1_{A^{\prime}}]\rangle

We can also show that this in fact an isomorphism, whose inverse is precisely the mix map,
\psi^{-1}_{A,B}=\mathrm{mix}_{A,B}
. To show this, we compute the following:

\displaystyle\iota^{0}_{A,A^{\prime}};\psi_{A,A^{\prime}};\psi^{-1}_{A,A^{\prime}}=~\langle 1_{A},a;b_{A^{\prime}}\rangle;\mathrm{mix}_{A,A^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=~\Delta_{A};(1_{A}\times a;b_{A^{\prime}});(1_{A}\times{u^{L}_{+}}^{-1}_{A^{\prime}});\delta^{L}_{A,\bot,A^{\prime}};((1_{A}\times m)+1_{A^{\prime}});({u^{R}_{\times}}^{-1}_{A}+1_{A^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=~\Delta_{A};(1_{A}\times a);(1_{A}\times\iota^{0}_{\bot,{A^{\prime}}});\delta^{L}_{A,\bot,{A^{\prime}}};((1_{A}\times m)+1_{A^{\prime}});({u^{R}_{\times}}^{-1}_{A}+1_{A^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=~\Delta_{A};(1_{A}\times a);\iota^{0}_{A\times\bot,{A^{\prime}}};((1_{A}\times m)+1_{A^{\prime}});({u^{R}_{\times}}^{-1}_{A}+1_{A^{\prime}})

(by Lemma
3.2
)

\displaystyle=~\Delta_{A};(1_{A}\times a);(1_{A}\times m);{u^{R}_{\times}}^{-1}_{A};\iota^{0}_{A,{A^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=~\Delta_{A};(1_{A}\times t_{A});{u^{R}_{\times}}^{-1}_{A};\iota^{0}_{A,{A^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=~1_{A};\iota^{0}_{A,{A^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=~\iota^{0}_{A,{A^{\prime}}}

and similarly
\iota^{1}_{A,A^{\prime}};\psi_{A,A^{\prime}};\psi^{-1}_{A,A^{\prime}}=\iota^{1}_{A,A^{\prime}}
. Therefore, by the couniversal property of the coproduct, we get that
\psi_{a,b};\psi^{-1}_{a,b}=1_{A+B}
. Similarly, we can show
\psi^{-1}_{a,b};\psi_{a,b}=1_{A\times B}
(instead using the universal property of the product). As such,
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
is a semi-additive category. Then
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
is a compact CLDC. Moreover, it not difficult to check that the induced linear distributors from the semi-additive structure end up being precisely the same linear distributors of
{\mathbb{X}}
(this is because the linear distributors were used to define the mix map).

\Box

Now for any CLDC
{\mathbb{X}}
, there is another equivalent way to describe objects in
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
. Indeed, recall from Lemma
3.6
that saying that
A\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
is equivalent to saying
A\cong A\times\bot
. Moreover, for every
B\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}
, clearly
B\times\bot\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
and so
(B\times\bot)\times\bot\cong B\times\bot
, meaning
B\times\bot\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
. Therefore, every object in
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
is isomorphic to one of the form
-\times\bot
. So let
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\times\bot
be the full sub-category of objects of the form
A\times\bot
for some
A
in
{\mathbb{X}}
, then:

Lemma 5.15

Let
{\mathbb{X}}
be a CDC. Then
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot\simeq\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\times\bot
, and therefore
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\times\bot
is a semi-additive category.

Proof:
Define the functor
F\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\times\bot
on objects as
F(A)=A\times\bot
and on maps as
F(f)=f\times 1_{\bot}
. It is clear that
F
is full, faithful, and essentially surjective. Therefore,
F
induces an equivalence
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot\simeq\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\times\bot
as desired. Explicitly, the functor
G\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\times\bot\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
is defined on objects as
G(A\times\bot)=A\times\bot
and
G(f)=f
. The natural isomorphism
\alpha_{A}:A\to GF(A)
is defined as
\alpha_{a}=\langle 1_{A},a\rangle
, while the natural isomorphism
\beta_{A\times\bot}:FG(A\times\bot)\to A\times\bot
is defined as
\beta_{A\times\bot}=\pi^{0}_{A\times\bot,\bot}
.

\Box

So from a CLDC, we obtain a subcategory that is semi-additive. Naturally, one can ask what happens if we apply the slice construction to a semi-additive category, which we now know is a compact CLDC. Well in this case, note that the initial object is a zero object and hence a terminal object. It is well-known that taking the slice category over the terminal is isomorphic to the original base category. Therefore, we have that:

Corollary 5.16

Let
{\mathbb{B}}
be a semi-additive category. Then
{\mathbb{B}}
is isomorphic to
\mbox{${\mathbb{B}}$}/\bot
.

Let us now explain why Frobenius cartesian linear functors preserve this construction. So let
F\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\to\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
be a Frobenius cartesian linear functor. Recall in particular this implies that we have an isomorphism
n_{\bot}:F(\bot)\to\bot
. Therefore, for any
A\in\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
, with associated map
a:A\to\top
, we get the composite
F(a);n_{\bot}:F(A)\to F(\bot)\to\bot
, which implies that
F(A)\in\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}/\bot
. As such, this allows us to restrict
F
to this subcategory, so we get a functor
F/\bot\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}/\bot
which is simply defined as
F/\bot(-)=F(-)
. We now explain how this is in fact a semi-additive functor.

Proposition 5.17

Let
F\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}\to\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}
be a Frobenius cartesian linear functor. Then
F/\bot\,\colon\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot\rightarrow\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}/\bot
is a semi-additive functor.

Proof:
By the explanation given above,
F/\bot
is well-defined. Moreover, since
F
preserves (up to isomorphism) products, coproducts, and the initial object,
F/\bot
will as well. Since the initial object in
\mbox{${\mathbb{X}}$}/\bot
and
\mbox{${\mathbb{Y}}$}/\bot
is also the terminal object, it follows that
F/\bot
preserves the terminal object as well. It remains now to show that
F/\bot
satisfies (
30
). This is equivalent to checking the following equalities:

\displaystyle{n_{+}}_{A,B};\langle[1_{F(A)},F(b);n_{\bot};b_{F(A)}],[F(a);n_{\bot};b_{F(B)},1_{F(B)}]\rangle;{m_{\times}}_{A,B}

\displaystyle=F(\langle[1_{A},a;b_{A}],[f;b_{B},1_{B}]\rangle)

\displaystyle{m_{\times}}_{A,B};F(\mathrm{mix}_{A,B});{n_{+}}_{A,B}=\mathrm{mix}_{F(A),F(B)}

The former is equivalent to

\displaystyle\langle[1_{F(A)},F(b);n_{\bot};b_{F(A)}],[F(a);n_{\bot};b_{F(B)},1_{F(B)}]\rangle

\displaystyle={n^{-1}_{+}}_{A,B};F(\langle[1_{A},b;b_{A}],[a;b_{B},1_{B}]\rangle);{m_{\times}}^{-1}_{A,B}

which holds since

\displaystyle\iota^{i}_{F(A),F(B)};{n^{-1}_{+}}_{B};F(\langle[1_{A},b;b_{A}],[a;b_{B},1_{B}]\rangle);{m_{\times}}^{-1}_{A,B};\pi^{j}_{F(A),F(B)}

\displaystyle=F(\iota^{i}_{A,B};\langle[1_{A},b;b_{A}],[a;b_{B},1_{B}]\rangle;\pi^{j}_{A,B})

and
F(c);b_{B}=F(c);n_{\bot};b_{F(B)}
for any
c\,\colon C\rightarrow\bot
. The latter holds by commutativity of the diagram in Figure (
1
), where the
(*)
square commutes as
\top
is terminal (or
\bot
is initial) and therefore
n_{\bot}^{-1};F(m);m_{\top}^{-1}=m\,\colon\bot\rightarrow\top
.

\textstyle{F(A)\times F(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{m_{\times}}_{A,B}}
\scriptstyle{1_{F(A)}\times{u^{L}_{+}}^{-1}_{F(B)}}
\scriptstyle{1_{F(A)}\times F({u^{L}_{+}}^{-1}_{B})}
\textstyle{F(A\times B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{({\rm nat})}
\textstyle{F(A\times(\bot+B))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
